COMMITTEE ON INTERIOR AND INSULAR AFFAIRS JAMES E. MURRAY, Montana, Chairman CLINTON P. ANDERSON, New Mexico HENRY M. JACKSON, Washington JOSEPH C. O'MAHONEY, Wyoming ALAN BIBLE, Nevada RICHARD L. NEUBERGER, Oregon JOHN A. CARROLL, Colorado FRANK CHURCH, Idaho GEORGE W. MALONE, Nevada ARTHUR V. WATKINS, Utah HENRY DWORSHAK, Idaho THOMAS H. KUCHEL, California FRANK A. BARRETT, Wyoming BARRY GOLDWATER, Arizona GORDON ALLOTT, Colorado RICHARD L. CALLAGHAN, Chief Clerk N. D. MCSHERRY, Assistant Chief Clerk ROBERT E. WOLF, Forest Consultant II

MEMORANDUM OF THE CHAIRMAN To Members of the Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs: 

During early 1958 numerous people in the West, Senator Jackson, Senator Neuberger and other Senators called to my attention Forest Service timber sale problems. Senator Jackson ably pointed out on the Senate floor on August 16, 1958, the budgets submitted by the executive branch for national forest timber sales had been cut substantially below Forest Service estimates of funds needed in 4 of the last 5 fiscal years. Sale programs that had been circulated to timber companies have not been fulfilled. As the Senator from Washington said: *** promises cannot be sawed in a sawmill, chipped at a pulp plant, and peeled in veneer mills. *** 

After informal discussions with members of the committee and members of the Appropriations Committee, it was decided to have a staff study made in order that the 86th Congress would have before it an up-to-date picture. 

Several salient problems are effectively highlighted in this staff report. There is a need to develop a long-range goal for national forest timber programs. Recognition must be given to the lead time required to efficiently prepare an adequate number of timber sales. 

To improve service to the forest industry and to assure the best type of forest management there must be a full "pipeline" of planned sales. 

There is a significant opportunity to increase timber harvest by salvaging accessible trees now destroyed by forest insects and disease. 

This will improve the forest and recover losses not now in sustained yield quotas. A larger opportunity to market timber in sustained yield quotas can be met if roads can be provided into inaccessible areas.

In addition to inadequate budget requests, the program has been hindered because several hundred million board-feet of proposed timber sales have been tied up by the absence of an active, firm Department of Agriculture policy on Government access to its own. national forests. The Forest Service has not always been capable of making a timely presentation of essential facts revealing various problems on and affecting timber sales. It has not regularly gathered and analyzed information on all the various important elements of the timber-sale program.

I know that you share with me the view that despite the problems that exist and in spite of the real obstacles it has faced, the Forest Service has done a commendable job in resources management. 

I hope that this report will receive your careful consideration and that you will make it available to interested people. I shall await with interest your suggestions on the further steps that we should consider to help meet the conservation challenge that confronts our national forests now and in the years that lie ahead. 

JAMES E. MURRAY, Chairman.

LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL Senator JAMES E. MURRAY, Chairman, Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs, 

Washington, D. C. 

DEAR SENATOR MURRAY: This letter transmits a review of national forest timber sales in three western regions made at your direction in the fall of 1958. The review actually included Region 4 and one forest in it, the Boise in Southern Idaho. The limited work I did in this region caused me to omit it from the report. The problems, however, are quite similar to those in Region 1.

As you directed, I have gone over the facts presented in the report with the Forest Service and we are in agreement on them. There are, of course, some minor differences on their interpretation. The conclusions and recommendations have been reviewed by the Forest Service, but their views could only be properly expressed after further study. During the entire study I had excellent cooperation and assistance from all of the people in the Forest Service. 

The national forests are multiple-use public lands, and to be successfully administered as such each of the programs must operate not only as intended, but also in harmony with other uses. The values of these federally managed forests cannot be measured alone by timber sold or dollars of income to the Treasury. The accomplishments of the Forest Service must be evaluated not just on quantity, but in terms of the less easy to detect, though more important quality of the work. If the quality of the work measures up to the demands of the future, then conservation goals will be certain of achievement.

Though these facts are not reiterated in the report, I did give constant consideration to them as I proceeded. 

Sincerely yours, ROBERT E. Wolf,

Forest Consultant.

REVIEW OF  IN THREE WESTERN REGIONS INTRODUCTION 

The review covered the part of region 1 embracing Montana, Idaho, and Washington; the part of region 4 covering southern Idaho; region 6 which covers Washington and Oregon; and region 5 which covers California. These 4 regions account for about 75 percent of the allowable cut potential in the national-forest system exclusive of the Alaska forests. In addition, the forests selected for sampling within the regions contained several of those with the largest timber business. 

Inspections were made at 3 regional offices-1, 5, and 6, and, in addition, at 16 national forests. These included the Lolo, Flathead, and 

Kootenai in Montana, the Kaniksu and Boise in Idaho, the Gifford 

Pinchot, Snoqualmie, Wenatchee, and Okanagan in Washington, the

Mount Hood, Willamette, Umpqua, and Siskiyou in Oregon, and the 

Klamath, Shasta-Trinity and the Eldorado in California.

Prior to the commencement of the field inspection, the Washington office of the Forest Service furnished certain selected information on the overall timber sale program. Each region to be visited provided general regional information and a forest by forest report on annual timber sales accomplishments listing each proposed sale since 1955. 

On the basis of an analysis of this information, the above-listed forests were selected for inspection. The purpose was to obtain a good cross sectional picture which would include forests which on the basis of the record, were doing a better than average job, as well as to visit those forests which were experiencing problems. 

The on-the-ground review for the selected forests consisted of going over the timber-sale program in detail with the Forest Supervisor, the timber-management staff and, in some cases, the district rangers. 

This review covered the reasons for delay or postponement on each planned sale and consideration of the problems of meeting sale goals.

Certain additional analyses were made on the spot to determine the number and type of sales in the process of preparation, sales on which preparatory work had been done in the past but which would not materialize, problems in sales programing including rights-of-way, road engineering, timber management plans, and personnel problems, an analysis of timber under contract and the origin of cutting requirements in relation to the year when sales are made, the relation between planned and actual sale size and the effect upon financed programs, and recordkeeping techniques with special reference to how well present records assure current knowledge on whether financed program goals would be met.

The review at the regional office covered the forests not visited and an overall analysis of the regional picture based upon the facts revealed by the data supplied by the Forest Service. 

All of the personnel of the Forest Service were most cooperative and helpful, spending considerable time over and above the normal workweek to facilitate the review. The task was helped especially by the warm assistance and knowledgeable aid provided by Ben O. 

Hughes, Assistant, Division of Timber Management, Washington office. 1

SUMMARY 

In order to view in perspective the Forest Service timber-sale program it is well at the outset to examine briefly the changes that have taken place over the years.

This table illustrates the growth of the national forest timber-sale business.

Except for the depression years there has been a generally steady growth in the volume of timber sold and cut. Some of the unusual increases in timber sold in various years resulted from large long-term sales. Timber cut has shown a steadier growth better reflecting the real availability of timber sold. The phenomenal aspect is the tremendous increase in the value of timber cut which was $2.20 per thousand board-feet in 1920 and stayed at about this level until 1940 when it rapidly climbed to $8.50 per thousand board-feet by 1950 then to $14.50 per thousand board-feet by 1958. Timber cut climbed from under 1 billion board-feet in 1920 to 3.5 billion board-feet by 1950. 

Then up to almost 7.0 billion board-feet by 1958. 

With such a great growth both in volume and value in recent years it is evident that national forest timber is increasing in importance. 2 It

is neither surprising that there have been problems nor that with the increase in business some of these problems have become more difficult while others have been solved.

This report does not deal with the problems that have been successfully solved nor does it dwell upon accomplishments. The record, and the generally high regard which the public have for the Forest Service, make this unnecessary. Rather it reviews the one particular segment of the Forest Service program which has accounted for 86 percent of the $1 billion in cash income which the national forests have deposited in the Treasury since 1905. The review is an effort to identify the steps that may be helpful to making the record better in the years that lie ahead.

The review was made at this time because groups in the forest products industry alleged that the Forest Service was not adhering to published timber sale announcements furnished the industry and that there was a substantial gap between timber sales and allowable cuts. 

Additionally groups indicated concern about other elements of the timber-sale program such as sale size, opportunities to salvage timber, access roads, and right-of-way needs. 

The field review was conducted to assess both the validity and dimensions of complaints and to determine their cause. 

The review showed the following record for the three regions. studied in detail.

The above tables show the volume of timber that the regional offices estimated would be sold or cut with funds provided and the actual record expressed as a percentage each year when the funds are allotted the Washington office first sets quotas. Regions 1 and 6 usually set forest quotas which in sum were for about the same total volume as the Washington quota. Region 5, however, set selling quotas which for the 4-year period show a performance record of 75 percent against one of 91 percent when measured against the Washington office quota. 

Region 5's optimism on accomplishments has not been matched by its sales record.

The record on timber sold is well below financed levels except for region 6. While regions 1 and 6 have shown improvement, region 5 has not. The industry cut about 90 percent of the financed cut in each region but the Forest Service did not offer as large a percentage of sales as they were financed to handle. On a quantity basis the volume sold has exceeded the industry's removal of timber except in region 1 where the reverse is true. 

The record based on financed plans is reasonably good. It masks the fact that if the industry had cut the full amount of timber the Forest Service estimated it would cut there would have been a further drain on funds and less money would have been available for sale preparation. The recent fair sales record in regions 1 and 5 and the good record in region 6 would have fallen to a less satisfactory level.

In region 6 when funds were reapportioned sales goals were revised upward and cutting goals downward in fiscal year 1958. This had the effect of reducing sales performance percentages while raising cutting performance percentages. However, the region was also financed to prepare for sale an additional 800 million board-feet for future years. Immediate sales goals were only 94 percent met and cutting was 91 percent of the revised goal.

Sales goals for fiscal year 1959 are 3,802.9 million board-feet compared to 4,150 million for 1958 while cut goals have risen in 1959 to 3,534.5 million board-feet compared to 2,995 million board-feet in 1958.

Revised goals for 1958 were not met and the position of the 800 million board-feet in additional sales preparation is not readily ascertainable. This backlog of partially prepared sales should be helpful in meeting 1959 sales goals.

In addition, measured against available data on announced plan accomplishments, region 6 sold only 79 percent and region 1 49 percent. It was the failure to adhere to timber sales plans announced on the various national forests plus an inability to offer sales at full allowable cut levels which are the focal points of industry complaints.

The Forest Service did announce annual sales plans for volume substantially above these subsequently made. Except in region 1 (Montana and north Idaho) the announced plans have been for volumes well above the level of sales activity financing. The closer to the forest, the more optimistic forest officers are about the timber sales volume that can be processed. To some extent this represents an effort to respond to local demands for timber and the hope of obtaining a larger share of each region's allotment to meet this demand. 

It also stems from a realization that not all scheduled sales will materialize due to technical problems.

Among the major obstacles found to marketing announced nationalforest timber sales were cancellations which occurred because needed rights-of-way and cost-sharing agreements had not been obtained from intermingled landowners who generally are members of the forest products industry. Another obstacle is the cost of needed mainline roads required. When timber purchasers are required to build the needed road, particularly in timber stands with marginal value species salability is diminished. Financing for timber sales has not been adequate to build a backlog of prepared sale so that varying market conditions can be met. Currently when an obstacle to planned sale cannot be overcome a new sale must be layed out if program goals are to be met.

Forest Service timber-sales programs rest on decade revised timbermanagement plans which describe overall management goals on the forest, annually revised 5-year Cutting Budgets which attempt to define and locate general sale areas, and an annual Timber Business Report which combines a budget request for the fiscal year 2 years off with a request for an allotment from the next year's budget. 

There is no common anniversary date for timber-management plans thus they provide only a broad guide for management. The 

5-year Cutting Budgets do not have a common anniversary date either but they can be coordinated, while the annual timber business reports afford a current device for fiscal coordination. Until recently these reports showed timber to be cut but did not show timber to be sold. 

This has been a basic weakness in operations planning and budget presentations.

The volume and rate of timber cutting is largely affected by the economic situation of the industry, the extent to which alternate log supplies are used, the size of contracts, roads needed to permit cutting and the operating pattern of customers. It is the element which the 

Forest Service has made the least effort to consciously direct but it has been the biggest element used to relate fund requirements to program goals.

The Forest Service timber-sale program really consists of three interdependent parts: 

1. The presale activity which leads up to programing sales for detailed preparation work; 

2. The actual layout of the timber for sale and its advertisement; and 

3. The supervision of sales under contract.

In the timber-sale business, the future is governed by the present, but the present is governed by the past. An effective timber-sale program requires that the three elements move forward in harmony. 

The volume under contract creates an inescapable workload which

influences the amount of presale preparation and actual sale layout work can be accomplished. The sales activity determines the workload in the future and the size and conditions of sales made coupled with the desires of purchasers influences when the timber will be cut. 

There is no publically set forth long-range program which the Congress or the public can examine to determine the goals that the Department of Agriculture has for national forest timber management. 

This review of current problems in the national forest timber-sale program must be considered against this background because the operational and budgetary difficulties that presently exist stem from the lack of a clear goal with quantitative accomplishments related to time.

Finally the Forest Service annual plans and accomplishments reports do not presently give a complete picture of the operational problems or a quantitative assessment of situations requiring policy consideration.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 

The problems found in this study are cumulative and growing because the basic tools for their solution have not been fully utilized. 

The study made leads to these conclusions and recommendations: 

1. NATIONAL PLAN NEEDED 

In the public mind the Forest Service timber-sale program has as its only announced goal reaching something called the "full allowable cut" at the earliest practical date. Because of the rapidly growing importance of the national forests, as a timber source, for recreation and other uses there is an urgent need for definite management goals, fixed to a point in time, explained as to objective, and relating operating and investment requirements to benefits. The Department of 

Agriculture has just completed publication of Timber Resources for America's Future, the most complete analysis of the Nation's timber resources that has been made. This review includes forecasts of timber supply and demand both for the years 1975 and 2000. 

A national plan should be made setting forth forest-management goals for the future which adequately describes- (a) Timber-harvesting goals; (b) Reforestation and silvicultural work needed to attain optimum forest production; (c) Recreation, watershed protection, grazing goals, and landuse adjustments needed to harmonize multiple uses; (d) Estimates of the role the national forest will play in the 

Nation's economy; (e) Road and servicing facilities needed for management and operation.

Cost estimates to reach stated goals within the first 5-year period, as well as overall costs.

The overall program should be utilized to formulate short-terin goals and as the background for budget requests. Special attention should be given to developing presentations which evaluate how program goals are being met. 

2. LACK OF ACCESS HELD UP SALES 

The major factor holding up planned timber sales in the period studied was lack of rights-of-way. This includes obtaining bare-land rights-of-way in order to construct a Forest Service road, purchase of the right to use an existing road, and agreements for cooperative construction of new roads serving two or more ownerships. Information furnished in Region 6 showed that over one billion board feet in unmade or delayed planned sales was due to these problems. In

Region 1 over 500 million board feet in sales delays stemmed from the same source. In addition, there are substantial volumes ready to put into sales that never enter plans because of unresolved right-ofway difficulties. While on the one hand, it is a fact that the Forest 

Service has not been financed to sell all the timber that theoretically could be sold, on the other, it is clear that the Service, under present policies, has expended considerable time in fruitless negotiations where it is unlikely that agreements will be forthcoming. 

Policies have not been set forth and backed up with funds so as to enable the Service to utilize the full range of possibilities in negotiations. Thus, the United States is at a disadvantage in negotiating both with parties who proceed in good faith, as well as with those who do not.

Progress has been made under the present "ground rules" but the true measure is the relation of this cause of delay to other causes and its effect on the sale program. Timber management is not possible without permanent access arrangements. 

The Department of Agriculture should set forth a right-of-way policy and an active adequately financed program geared to solving problems in an orderly, firm and fair manner. 

3. INADEQUATE RECORDS 

Forest Service records on timber sales are not sufficient to reveal significant and important facts on a timely basis for its $100 million annual business. The Forest Service has emphasized "timber cut" as an expression of timber-sales accomplishments. This measure is incomplete and misleading because it fails to properly describe accomplishments. The Service should clearly show the entire program and the interrelationships that exist. The minimum elements of the sale program that should be included are- (a) The volume of timber in the various stages of sale preparation. (b) Sales planned for the coming year with appropriate information on sale-size components, the cost of accessory timber purchaser roads, and the anticipated rate at which the sales will actually be "loggable." (c) The volume of timber under contract and the rate at which it will be logged in the forthcoming year. 

These key records, adequately briefed and highlighted for use by the Chief, the Department, the Bureau of the Budget, and the Congress, should form the nucleus of the budget justification for timber sales, and would be the basic tool for timber-sale policy and program decisions.

Adequate facts would then be available to aid in securing necessary funds to maintain a suitable backlog of work in the various stages of preparation, to maintain an even flow of sales from preparation into production, to gear sales to meeting the requirements of prospective customers, to assuring that sales preparation and administration work is of a high quality and that increases in timber cutting can be met without later jeopardizing other elements of the timber sale job.

4. CORRELATED STANDARDS DO NOT MEASURE CURRENT COSTS AND SALES PLANS NOT COORDINATED WITH FUNDS 

The Forest Service utilizes a detailed jobload analysis called Correlated Standards to determine field staffing levels, anticipated and expected work production, and funds needed to meet program goals. 

This procedure enables the Service to define its needs but it has often lagged in measuring current costs. In addition, it has not fully stated time requirements to do the entire timber sale job to current standards. 

Thus timber sale fund requests have not been sufficient to advance sales to the maximum level of operation. Revisions are now being made and the standards should be strengthened by local analysis and performance records.

Timber-sale plans are prepared on a long- and short-term basis, budgets are developed, funds are allotted, results are evaluated, but these actions have not been always closely related to each other on a timely and coordinated basis. Therefore it is not currently possible to quickly evaluate program condition and to effect timely improvements or detect emergent difficulties. 

The Forest Service has a highly decentralized operation. In this period of rapid growth of its timber-sale business it has not provided local officers with the management tools and guides needed to assure that the responsibility for performance is properly fixed at the local level. Work standards are on an average basis and do not properly show the range of time required on various ranger districts. In the timber-sale job there is a need for local officers to estimate man-day requirements for the various elements of each sale as a part of budget development. When funds are appropriated time estimates should be related to the planned work and time deadlines established for job completion. This record at the district level would permit the ranger to start his work planning at an earlier date, and to later adjust his program rapidly as experience showed variations from actually financed levels. The forest supervisor and regional office would be in a position to examine situations where planned time varied from work standards. The regional office could then better review proposed variations in formulating budget estimates. Subsequently the ranger, with the benefit of a better basic estimate should be able to more closely meet plans. Through its regular inspection system the Service would be in a position to quickly and effectively evaluate performance. 

Presently substantial volumes of information are prepared locally for regional use but a very limited portion is transmitted to the Washington level. Local records should be revised to make them into management tools, the volume of detail flowing to regional offices controlled and slightly more concise detail supplied to the Washington office along with evaluation summaries.

The budget officers should review the timber-sale program taking into account its economic implications and its revenue-producing potential. Performance measures should be evaluated to develop effective devices which will assure that estimates of needed funds for planned sales will be accurately reflected by accomplishments.

Arbitrarily setting the level of timber-sale funds will have increasingly serious implications for the growing segment of the forest industry dependent upon national-forest timber and for the communities in the 39 States where these forests are located. The keys to a successful timber-sale program are adequate lead times in sale preparation and a full-sale assembly line. 33630-59- —2

5. PERSONNEL TRANSFERS AND SALE RECORDS 

The lack of useful local records on the status of salework presents unique problems in a large organization which must frequently transfer personnel. Forest supervisors often pointed out that sale program efficiency was hampered by transfers. The extent to which there is validity to the point of view that transfers occur too often is one which personnel management should review. Some real savings might be made if it is found that the transfer rate is excessive. Greater use might also be made of short-term details to achieve the goal sought with lateral transfers. However, the ever-present need to transfer people should not be permitted to be used as a crutch to excuse shortcomings.

The study revealed that often new sales officers did not know the status of the work of their predecessor or what had happened to work previously planned. While it is understandable that some of the events surrounding an active contract would not be clear the Service now maintains a good record that starts when a timber contract is let. 

Proper record maintenance should start with the conception of a sale. 

If a sale aborts or is delayed there should be an adequate history showing cause, worktime expended, and salvable effort. 

More meaningful local records showing time estimates for each major element of the sale job, time expended, job deadlines, current sales status would help overcome the natural temporary loss of efficiency caused by transfers. But a more lasting benefit would be the month-to-month value in program administration. 

6. ANNOUNCED PLANS EXCEED TIMBER SOLD 

A major factor causing the current review was a consistent error on the part of region 6, Forest Service, in announcing annual timbersale plans with definite sale dates which were not then fulfilled either on schedule, for each of the listed sales, or in total quantity. This practice aggravated the industry, particularly those firms which did not have timber under contract and were in need of securing sales. The situation existed in other regions visited. 

These sale announcements were not closely coordinated with financed sale programs except in region 1. Corrective action has been taken by the Service. Care should be taken that future plans are absolutely related to realistic financed programs and that full public information is available on the exact cause for not currently offering the planned sale or for sale delays. 

7. ROADS NEEDED FOR PERMANENT CUT EXPANSION BUT SALVAGE PLANS CAN BE INCREASED NOW 

Over the years, the Forest Service, with rare exceptions, has not sold the full amount of timber available to sell under sound forestmanagement considerations. Originally this was due to a lack of real demand. During the thirties there was an active withholding of timber from the market at the industries' request. The net result was that in the forties when timber demand started to climb the Forest Service was not in a position to rapidly respond. Basic development which comes from an adequate road system had lagged behind even then current needs. Thus, still today there are real

limits to the rate at which timber sales can be expanded. Expansion via large sales carries with it having the timber purchaser construct the roads. This method does not permit a rapid annual increase in cutting commensurate with the total volume contracted nor will it quickly bring sale performance up to allowable cuts. In some cases the marginal value of species in the stand requires that the major road network be available to attract customers. 

The Forest Service carries forward to the next 5-year cutting budget a portion of the harvest deficit of the preceding period. The degree to which this deficit can be captured is controlled by both physical and fiscal limitations. There are substantial volumes where reasonable road construction will permit sale but unless additional road construction funds are made available, there will be fairly tight limitations on the rate at which sales can be expanded. If, however, main-line roads can be programed with appropriated funds into areas with usable carryovers in cutting budget deficits, not only can sales be expanded more rapidly, but the rate of return on road investment will be accelerated.

Beyond the allowable cut there is a volume of timber, excluded from cutting budgets, which is considered normal mortality loss due to age, insects, or disease. It is composed of scattered trees throughout the forest salvable only by small sales. Salvage sales in already roaded areas offer a quicker method of temporarily raising sales levels, although they do not solve the basic management problem. Where there is a road system "in" this timber can be readily marketed. It is timber that can be cut immediately. These sales fit the needs, buying habits, and financial ability of many small loggers. Because this volume will be "lost" if not salvaged, further because virtually no road investment, either public or private, is needed to cut it and finally because the return to the Treasury on direct cost will be 5 to 1, this opportunity to raise the total volume harvested should be diligently utilized. 

8. SALE SIZE AFFECTS COST TO MAKE SALES AND OPPORTUNITIES FOR FIRMS TO SECURE TIMBER 

The cost to the Forest Service for processing sales varies with sale size. Forest sales size plans and actual sales did not always agree. 

When actual sales are smaller than those planned, funds will not produce the same volume of timber marketed. When the shift is to larger sales the forest record should surpass plans. The Service should insure that substantial variations from financed plans are reflected in performance expected.

Considerable heat has been generated by industry spokesmen arguing both for and against large and small sales. If the objective is to sell timber at the lowest expenditure of appropriated funds, large sales would be the answer. If the objective is to provide the broadest opportunity to all segments of the industry to bid for timber and in the process to insure the fullest chance for price to be set by the industry, medium and small sales should predominate. Despite the somewhat higher expenditure for processing net sale income will also be larger. Even on small sales revenue is substantially above costs.

Sale size is an effective device whereby the Government may influence the composition of the industry in national forest areas and particularly may affect the life expectancy, the number and size of firms that do not own private timber reserves. The social and economic impact of sale size decisions constitute policy considerations that need further analysis and decision by the Department of Agriculture in developing the plan suggested in point 1. 

9. IMPROVED SALE AND ROAD PLANS 

A considerable volume in proposed sales had to be deferred and replanned because on the ground examination revealed errors in the original sale plan or estimate. Improved presale reconnaissance would help in firming up plans. 

One lumberman suggested that initial timber sale and road location planning could be conducted from the air with an appreciable cost and time saving. The increased availability of aerial photos and new mapping techniques may make it practical to conduct a pilot study comparing conventional sale selection methods to helicopter and light plane costs and results.

TIMBER SALES PROGRAMING 

There are three interdependent elements in the timber sale program: 

1. The presale activity which leads up to programing sales for detailed preparation work; 

2. The actual layout of the timber for sale and its preparation for advertisement; and 

3. The supervision of sales once placed under contract. 

In this last element, timber cutting by contractors is a major part and is controlled by them. It creates an inescapable workload which influences the first two elements of the timber-sale program. the same time it is the first two elements which control how much timber will be under contract.

At 

The problems the Forest Service has, and they are many, stem from difficulties in keeping these three elements of the program moving forward in harmony. This can be accomplished by adequate advance planning, translation of long-range plans into current action plans, financial budgets, and finally the measurement of the effectiveness with which plans are carried out. 

Before considering in detail the three elements of the sale program some explanation of the various steps in program formulation is essential.

Advance planning is accomplished by Forest Management Plans prepared every 10 years; by annually revised 5-year Cutting Budgets or Timber Action Plans which are part of the Forest Management 

Plan; and by the Timber Business Report, a short summary report which sets forth the current situation. 

boho TIMBER MANAGEMENT PLANS AND CUTTING BUDGETS 1 

The management plans are prepared in the field and come to both the regional and Washington office for approval. While plans can be read and the problems gleaned from it, the result is to obtain only one picture. The plans are not prepared on a fixed and common date for each forest and therefore common long-term operating and financing problems are not brought together through the device of the management plan. The goal is to revise each plan every 10 years. 

The present management plans are a mixture of historical information, geographic, topographic, climatic, and economic data, timber marking, allowable cut computations, silvicultural prescriptions, plus some analysis of major operating problems. 

They form the backdrop against which plans for a 5-year basis are set forth and considered on a more detailed basis. However, the extent to which major operating problems set forth in the management plan are resolved through Service or regional policy revisions, budget policy or legislation controls the scope, direction, and effectiveness of the 5-year cutting budget or timber sale action plan. 

1 Exhibit 1, p. 61; exhibit 2, p. 88. 13

This plan which is highly developed in region 6 is not only a 5-year plan but also a detailed action plan for the coming year. In tabular form it sets forth specific sales with volume to sell and cut as related to the allowable cut; expected timber to be sold and cut for the next 18 months; the 5-year access road plan both for timber purchaser and appropriated fund financed roads; a detailed justification for appropriated fund financing and inventory and management plan data. 

The regional action plan with detailed information for fiscal year 1960 which covers July 1959, through June 30, 1960, will be submitted in February 1959, to the regional office.

It is pertinent to note that the budget decisions of the executive branch for the fiscal year the plan will affect have just then been transmitted to the Congress and the effectiveness of the plan depends upon first, the action taken by the executive branch to seek the funds needed and, secondly, the action taken by Congress. 

TIMBER BUSINESS REPORT 2 

In fact, another report, the Timber Business Report, has progressed through agency channels and is a more controlling document since it not only precedes the action plan but also is used for financial budget purposes. This report sets for timber cut expectations, sale size groupings, listing cut and sell and costs as well as road needs. 

The report covering fiscal year 1960 was submitted in the spring of 1958 and was used for budget planning purposes in preparing the 1960 budget. It also was used to allocate fiscal year 1959 funds. 

The problem is that this series of reports masks the significant relationship that must exist between plans, time, and money. The timber management plan sets up broad, long-range timber-cutting goals but does not contain significant information on estimated manpower and financial needs to accomplish them. The management plan may set forth some of the operating problems confronting the forest but fail to associate them with the cost of their resolution or manpower needs.

The 5-year Cutting Budgets contain only volume to be cut, except in region 6 where a more specialized and expanded cutting budget develops substantial information on other phases of the sale program. 

Here again manpower requirements are not set forth. It is only in the timber business report that summarized dollar costs appear, and these are set forth in the overall requirements on a rigid basis which does not fully measure the three elements of the timber-sale program. 

In fact, until last year this report did not separate funds on a sell-andcut basis, but treated the entire subject on a timber-cut analysis. This is not to imply that the Forest Service has been unaware of its problems but rather to indicate that it has lacked regular quantitative assessments of its position so that the steps that must be taken to resolve emergent problems could be described in terms of dollars and manpower. 

2 Exhibits 3a and 3b, pp. 101-105.

CORRELATED STANDARD AND CONVERTING FACTORS FOR DETER- MINATION ON INDEX JOB LOAD WEIGHTS OF THE NATIONAL FORESTS 3 

The book with the above title is the key document in the formulation of Forest Service operations. Commencing in 1931, the Forest

Service started development of workload summaries for use in budget estimating, to achieve an equitable distribution of funds finally appropriated, to serve as a complete agency device for workload evaluation, to provide production and performance guidelines for organizational adjustments, manning requirements, classification of districts and assisting in management decisions. 

The standards are developed on a sample basis, and the hourly time allocations are converted to dollars for financial purposes. 

In practice, the timber management budget presented to the Chief of the Forest Service is developed by strict adherence to these standards. However, the subsequent action at the departmental level and the Bureau of the Budget does not recognize this basic working tool. In preparing the timber-sales budget each national forest estimates the volume of timber it will sell and cut, breaking the plan down into seven sales size classes and the various method of selling timber, such as scale sales with separate allocations for central or woods scale, or lump-sum sales. The volume proposed in each sale size class is multiplied by a dollar factor to derive the cost. In turn, the region summarizes the totals by sale size to present to the Chief of the Forest Service the regional program. 

The appendix contains the timber management section of the correlated standards, as well as examples of the timber business report which reflects forest and regional applications of the procedure.

The fiscal year 1958 program is briefly traced to show how the program is financed. The Timber Business Report is used to translate plans into funds. Cutting budget sales plans, and correlated standard time and cost factors are used to develop estimates of timber to be sold and cut by size classes. The program for the fiscal year starting 

July 1, 1957 (fiscal year 1958), was submitted to Washington in the spring of 1956. The field officers were generally required to apply standard cost factors from correlated standard tables in building the budget request. This was reviewed in the Washington office of the 

Forest Service and by the Secretary of Agriculture in the summer and presented to the Bureau of the Budget in the fall. By late 1956, this was crystallized into the President's budget. 

In January 1957, when the budget was released, the regions were advised on the funds requested. The Timber Business Report submitted in the spring of 1957, like its predecessor, set forth information for the fiscal year 1959 budget and also a request for an allotment of the fiscal year 1958 funds which was then before Congress.

The "functional" funds cover direct sales costs which cannot be allotted to the project sales by size, as well as indirect costs covering services performed by supporting divisions such as personnel and the general overhead functions performed by Washington regional and supervisors offices.

Both the project funds and the functional or base items are developed separately in budget preparation. The distribution is therefore proportional to the funds sought. The funds available, while 5 percent less than estimated as needed, were 32 percent higher than those available in 1957. Each region then made its allocations to the forest using the correlated standards as a base. The forest, generally by August 1 is in a position to make firm allocations to the ranger districts. In these allocations adjustments may be made to meet local conditions not adequately expressed by the correlated standards. 

However, since each district utilized the same standards to prepare the original estimate, opportunities to do this are limited. 

In August after the fiscal year 1958 funds were allotted, $500,000 was placed in budget reserve. This money was withdrawn from each region on a proportional basis and region 6's contribution was $180,000. 

In September $225,000 was released and it was all apportioned to region 6.

Exhibit 5 shows the original 1958 distribution of project funds by region 6 among its forests with an additional table showing the increased "sell" and "additional preparation" goal but not decreased cut goal, exhibit 6 shows the fiscal year 1955 allotment which included sale size tabulations, a feature not now used, and exhibit 7 shows the fiscal year 1959 project fund distribution.

On most of the forests that utilize public timber sale program announcements planning is on a calendar-year basis. The program is published in January or thereabouts. These usually state the size, location, and time of sales to be made. The announced program therefore has rested on a firm financial base for the first half of the calendar year. For the second half of each of the last 5 calendar years, with the exception of 1957 when funds were withdrawn but later increased for region 6, the program has had more funds available than in the first half of the calendar year. 

The balance of this section describes the interdependent elements of the timber sale program. Examples of some of the problems encountered in translating plans into performance are set forth and in the case history section the record in three Forest Service regions is reviewed.

THE PRESALE ACTIVITY 

The development of long-range timber sale plans including the resolution of major problems controlling their scheduled accomplishment is the first essential part of effective timber sale programing. 

Presale work, not only on overall sales programing, but also on general mainline road location and rights-of-way must be completed for large areas 2 or 3 years in advance of detailed initial sale layout. Overall sale programing includes the selection of sale size and location on a general basis taking into account the silvicultural needs for forest management and the economic possibilities of selling timber. In the many areas where the national forest timber is still in the initial development stage these problems take a considerable amount of time

which on the surface may seem to appear disproportionate to the amount of timber to be immediately sold. A drainage may have 800 million board-feet on perhaps 20,000 acres of national forest land and a current permitted annual cut of 9 million board-feet. The rightof-way solution may require an effort involving the entire drainage volume and area including an equal volume of non-Federal timber and possibly an even greater acreage of non-Federal land. The immediate outcome may be 3 major sales covering 27 million board-feet for an initial 3-year period but the real result is that at least 9 million boardfeet is made available for annual cutting in perpetuity and the 800 million board-feet of national forest timber is placed under active timber management.

The national forest timber management plan supposedly sets forth major matters to be considered in a long-range sale programing. 

It contains a general timber sale plan and the annually revised 5-year timber sale action plan. On one forest, where some sales delays have been experienced, the 1955 management plan states that, "there are no really serious road right-of-way problems". On the other hand the 1958 timber sale action plan for the forest lists 39 separate rightsof-way covering 20.3 miles of road which are needed to meet sales plans. One of the major factors listed covering inability to meet past sales plans in response to the committee inquiry was "right-ofway delays."

Similarly this forest is one where the stand per acre often necessitates a very large sale if road construction is to be by the timber purchaser. The management plan calls for very few access roads to be constructed with appropriated funds. The sale preparation record shows that difficulties have been experienced in putting up planned sales because the timber volume per acre was not sufficient to put in the basic road system. This has led to delays, and the readjustment of planned sales as well as sale plan cancellation. The initial development of a road system with appropriated funds would make it possible for sales to be made on a sound silvicultural basis. As matters now stand the necessity of putting sufficient volume in the sale to meet initial road costs affects forest planning. From the economic side the ability of various mills to secure timber is affected. There are 26 mills in the area served by this forest, 15 of which have been in business for more than 10 years. However, if only sales large enough to amortize initial development road costs are planned it may reduce potential bidders to the two largest mills in the area. On this forest in 1955 it was estimated that in the next decade 275.7 miles of road construction and reconstruction were required at a total cost of $3,223,525. New construction was estimated at an average cost of $14,100 per mile and reconstruction at an average of $6,200 per mile. 

The average cost for construction and reconstruction will be over $12 per thousand board-feet based on the cut planned for the decade if charged only to the higher value per species or over $6 if charged to all species.

The silvicultural necessity of cutting only about 30 percent of the stand and the difficulty in assessing heavy road costs against low-value species as a condition of a timber sale means that there are serious economic obstacles to overcome if the planned sale objectives are to be met. Thus it can be readily seen that there are a host of problems where long-range planning, including estimates of costs involved, is

essential to reaching a situation where current-year estimates of actual sales accomplishments can be reasonably firm. 

Presently the development of timber sale cost estimates rests upon a firm base known as "Correlated Standards and Converting Factors for the Determination of Index Job Load Weights." The present standards allocate a relatively small part of the project timber-sale effort to the job designated as "sale preparation" which includes examination of the area to determine silvical needs and marking practices for the proposed sale, estimates of timber volume and value, logging costs, stand improvement and slash treatment, road location, specification, costs, right-of-way deeds, and sale-area posting and surveys.

The basic weakness in the recent past is that the correlated standards themselves treated all jobs on a per-thousand-board-foot basis when is is doubtful that this is always a properly descriptive basis. For long-term basic sales planning acreage might be a better medium for expression. Road planning very probably could be better expressed in terms of miles of road while access problems could be better expressed in a case basis with adequate provision for recognizing degrees of complexity. A major part of the presale element of the timbersale job does not show up under present accomplishment reports as does timber cut and sold.

However, it is this element of the program which dictates future performance. It is therefore important that both in planning and budgeting adequate consideration be given to the nature and extent of the presale task and to devising adequate measurement of goals, and accomplishments.

DETAILED SALE LAYOUT 

The second step in a smoothly functioning program is the detailed sale layout needed to get the sale on the market. This job includes final sale area boundary determinations, a cruise of sufficient intensity to value the timber, detailed road location, and logging-cost estimates, timber appraisal, advertisement, and award of the sale. This element may include timber marking on a sale, in whole or in part. 

Timber management, as distinct from liquidation logging, requires attention in sale layout to the steps needed to assure prompt regeneration of a timber stand. Permanent forest cropping calls for permanent forest roads thus adequate engineering with provision for proper location and drainage becomes essential. Increased timber-management needs with greater emphasis on assuring prompt stand regeneration has required greater care in developing logging plans. 

Between 1945 and 1956 alone there was a 120-percent increase in the volume of national-forest timber sold and a 650-percent increase in the price received for this timber. The price of stumpage has risen more rapidly than the price of forest products. These facts indicate that it is essential both to the Government and to the forest products industry that a higher quality sale preparation job be done than was previously necessary. The 1955 report of this Committee on Federal Timber Sales Policies called attention to the need to increase the quality of sale preparation work. Under the pressure to make sales the Federal agencies had offered timber on the market without requiring that certain key field jobs be performed to minimum technical standards.

A further significant point is that there has been relatively little advancement in field techniques and those which have produced time savings have not even offset the need to increase standards of performance due to timber value changes. In contrast with other operations where labor efficiencies have provided substantial efficiency increases, the timber-sale field has remained virtually static. 

Thus in considering this element of the sale program recognition must be given the necessity of increasing the quality of the work. 

Nor can there be overlooked the fact that in response to demand, both economic and social, the size of timber sales has been lessened. 

Currently about 40 percent of the timber-sales volume in the Northwest are under 10 million board-feet, 30 percent are in sales between 10 million and 25 million board-feet, 20 percent sales between 25 million and 50 million board-feet, and 10 percent in sales over 50 million board-feet.

It is neither possible or desirable to attempt to designate one portion of the sale job as more important than another but it is essential to understand the interrelationship that exists between the various elements. The success of the sale-by-sale preparation work is primarily dependent upon how well the presale element is handled. 

The field study showed that in many cases failure to make planned sales resulted primarily from conditions which were not thoroughly explored in the presales period. It would serve no useful purpose in this report to reproduce the entire forest-by-forest record but the situation can be succinctly summed up. Right-of-way problems were a prime cause of sale delay and deferrals from as little as 3 months to several years were found. In low-value timber areas detailed examination often showed that the timber would not carry the road needed for long-term development or the timber was of marginal value in the current market. In some of these cases substantial detailed field work was done before a determination was made that a sale was not feasible. 

The Forest Service has a reasonably good record of planned sale accomplishment and the majority of planned sales are made as planned.

There are some operational problems that contribute to deficiencies in performance. The most effective period for detailed timber sale layout is in the summer months. At this time of year logging is at its peak and priority must be given to servicing existing contracts. In addition this is the peak fire season and recreation load period. 

Therefore, particularly in the case of contract administration and fires, if is it necessary to concentrate upon these activities, sale layout must be deferred. The result not only is delay but also the fieldwork is put over to a period when inclement weather forces up the time. required to complete the work. Thus it can be seen that work deferred often means reduced efficiency. The essentially seasonal nature of the program creates staffing problems which are of real magnitude. 

Another factor which affects management is the cost of sale layout in areas not serviced by roads. In these cases considerable time is utilized in transporting both road locators and timber sales personnel to and from remote locations. For example one sales area was visited where the sale layout had to be made during the winter under deep snow conditions in an not served by roads. One only had area to stand on the sale area and look down the mountain to the canyon bottom to realize that the time expended in getting to this fairly

large sale was bound to be considerably more than it would have been if the work could be done in a roaded area during the summer months. 

The real demands of other elements of the program and the tendency of time demands to peak at the period for most effective fieldwork are significant aspects in evaluating performance. In some sales the 

Forest Service marks all of the timber in advance of the sale, in others a sample of the area is marked prior to sale and the balance is marked just in advance of cutting. At the present time the major portion of the marking is done after the sale is made, and this practice has created operating problems. In some cases there are disputes about the marking, in others the purchaser will revise logging plans and defer cutting timber just marked. These and other changes will increase workloads at critical times. 

CONTRACT SUPERVISION AND ADMINISTRATION 

This final phase of timber sale programing has been the one which in the past has probably consumed more of the timber sale dollar than the presale or detailed layout work. More important it is also the element which cannot be deferred because the forest products industry essentially controls the rate and time of logging. The Forest Service, must provide the scaling services, the timber marking and the contract inspection and the subsequent slash disposal and timber stand improvement work must be scheduled as the active logging is completed. There has been industrial criticism on sale performance and a full understanding of the entire situation requires consideration of all of the factors that effect performance. 

Records kept by region I show that the industry varies considerably its removal of forest products from the national forests. On a regionwide basis for a recent 10-year period 35 percent of the timber was cut in the first quarter of the fiscal year, July, August, September. The variations between years are between 29% and 44 percent. In 3 consecutive years the cut was 37½, 29, and 42 percent. Similarly the second quarter of the year shows an average cut of 34 percent while in the third and fourth quarter each accounted for 15 percent.

In the second quarter their were variations between 26 and 40 percent in the first quarter the variations were between 7 and 21 percent while in the fourth quarter the variation was from 11 to 30 percent. A monthly tabulation would doubtless reveal that December in the second quarter and April in the fourth quarter were months of generally low demand along with the entire third quarter. The variations between forests are quite startling. For one, large forest, the Clearwater, with an average cut for the period of 30,000,000 board feet and a current cut in excess of 70,000,000 board feet there have been extreme variations. While in overall averages this forest looks like one reasonably close to the regional average it is difficult to find even one year that closely resembles either the forest or regional average. 

In one year only eight-tenths of 1 percent of the timber was cut in the first quarter while in the next it climbed to 65 percent. In the year when the first quarter was at a record low the industry cut 83 percent in the second quarter as compared to the forest long-term average for that quarter of 31 percent and the regional second quarter average of 

34 percent. 

4 Exhibit 8, p. 130.

The forest products industry has its seasonal aspects which influence employment and the very nature of the industry intensifies the seasonal aspects of the timber sale program of the Forest Service in the West. One scaler may be able to handle up to 100,000 board-feet a day at a station but any regular amount over that might require 2 scalers. Similarly if logging is concentrated on a portion of sales on a district one forester may be able to handle the inspection and marking while if logging is spread over most of the contracts the travel factor may require the assignment of 2 persons even though there is very little difference in the total volume removed. In addition contractors can convert four 8-million-board-foot sales into the equivalent of eight 4-million-board-foot sales by the rate at which they elect to operate them. This is an important consideration because until recently fund allocations under the correlated standard were on the basis of gross sale size rather than on the basis of rate of operation.

In the case of a mark and scale sale in region I the smaller unit sale may cost 40 percent more to service than the larger sale. At the same time it is pertinent to note that the Forest Service sale size breakdown lumps together sales of the 5 million to 15 million board-foot class. There is a 10 million board-foot sale size spread at a critical part of the size class designation and it is probable that the sales at the lower end of this class are somewhat more expensive to process than are those at the higher end. 

Several factors then would seem to be operating to cause the correlated standard allocation to understate the requirements for sales administration. The peaking of operations by the industry during certain times of the year; shifts in intensity of logging of a constantly varying nature; spreading operations to several contract areas as well as an unduly heavy concentration upon a few sale areas can all create time demands which curtail efforts to process proposed sales for the market.

This can only be partially corrected by securing better estimates from the industry of its plans for contract operation. 

SUMMARY 

The timber sale program is composed of three interrelated elements-presale planning, detailed sale layout, and sale administration. The obligation to service existing contracts takes the first priority in the program. Essential detailed timber sale layout work must often be shifted because of fire-control demands or accelerated timber cutting on existing contracts. In the former case timber project funds are still available but use is deferred until less favorable working periods while in the latter case the result is to curtail work on presale work or detailed sale layout. The effectiveness of detailed sale layout depends heavily in the extent to which adequate preliminary work can be done on proposed sales. Key factors are early resolution of right-of-way problems, reasonably accurate assessments of the size sale needed for the road required, and effective analysis of the market situation under which less valuable species may be marketed.

Detailed sale layout accomplishments are often reduced due to the need to reschedule work to a time when efforts are less productive due to the weather factor.

Preliminary sale preparation, including essential long-range plans based on operating, silvicultural, and economic considerations, is the weakest element in the present sales program. As a result the Forest 

Service is unable to properly capitalize on time expended on detailed sale layout and it lacks sale flexibility because it is unable to rapidly substitute sales when originally planned sales cannot be made.

3. 

CASE HISTORIES 

On the ground examinations were made on 16 national forests to determine the problems associated with the timber sale program. 

Reprinting the mass of data accumulated would not be an aid to clearly presenting the situation. Therefore, one detailed example is set forth as a case history basis, and regional summaries of sale programs are also included to provide a full picture. 

In order to obtain a balanced picture, the Forest Service was asked to provide certain records for calendar years 1955, 1956, 1957, and the first half of 1958. This data included the announced calendar year sales plans distributed to the public. In region 6 these are derived from the 5-year action plan, a regional report which is a part of the management plan for each forest. Each forest also listed all sales announced in plans, plus additional sales made during a year, and for each sale not made the reasons were stated.

These were reviewed on each forest visited, and the forests not visited were reviewed with regional officials. The timber business report (which is on a fiscal year basis) was obtained. An analysis was made of planned sale size, distribution, accomplishments, and performance in relation to budgeted funds. 

Timber volumes under contract were examined, and a tabulation was made of the contract year of origin for the timber cut for a sample year. Estimates were made of the status of preparatory work. The various forest officials were asked to comment on their estimate of the situation and the nature of the problems they have. 

The various examples are common to all regions to reduce the bulk of material they were illuminated by discussion in only 1 or 2 regions. 

THE SISKIYOU NATIONAL FOREST-REGION 6 

This national forest has an allowable cut of 153 million board-feet. 

In fiscal year 1945 the forest sold 40.8 million board-feet and timber operators cut 34.6 million board-feet. By fiscal year 1958 sales had risen to 178.4 million board-feet, an increase of 340 percent, and the cut had risen to 92.0 million board-feet, an increase of but 166 percent.

FACTORS AFFECTING FIGURES 

Before examining the causes for failure to achieve planned goals, a further explanation of the bare figures is in order. Exhibit 9 lists all of the sales planned and unplanned and gives various totals. 

One of the difficulties in reconciling the figures is that there is a difference between planned and actual volumes. The planned figure is generally preliminary estimate. For example, on the Galice and 

Illinois Valley Districts in 1956, 8 planned sales contained an estimated 26,655 thousand board-feet. When preparation was complete, 31,305 thousand board-feet or 18 percent more volume was advertised. 

However, generally on this forest there has been a slight decrease in volume sold as compared to planned volume on a sale-by-sale basis. 

When sales are carried over beyond the year planned there often has been a problem in making the sale large enough to finance the needed road. For example, one sale was increased from 15,000 thousand board-feet to 23,300 thousand board-feet. Thus actual sales volumes are not fully reconcilable with planned volumes.

Financed cut levels were raised while the actual cut has been fairly constant for the last 3 fiscal years. With cut lagging behind financed levels, it seems logical to expect sale performance to exceed financed levels.

A drop in timber cut does not automatically release personnel for other salework. Good analytical data is not available, but the general review indicates that cut can drop substantially without any appreciable saving of overall manpower requirements.

Thus the preparatory backlog was depleted, and it is unlikely now that this forest has improved its net sales capability position over what it was in 1956 or 1957. In 1957 only 51.5 thousand board-feet or 25 percent of the sales made came out of the "prepared" backlog of 202.3 thousand board-feet. In addition, 21,250 thousand board-feet of the remaining volume is in three sales, Spud Patch, Rainbow Creek and Lawson Creek planned in 1958 but not made.

One of these sales, Rainbow, was listed as 100 percent prepared on January 1, 1958. It was not offered in 1958 and has been increased in volume from 7,000 thousand board-feet to 17,000 thousand boardfeet. This is illustrative of a defect in the present procedure for cataloging the status of preparatory work. 

The forest had listed 14 separate sales totaling 127 thousand boardfeet for sale during the first half of calendar 1959. Three sales totaling 13 thousand board-feet were moved to the 1958 program of which one has been canceled, and only the Butler Creek sales noted above is planned for sale from the listed backlog. 

Thus in order to meet plans for the first half of 1959, about 100 million board-feet of new preparatory work had to be carried through to some stage of completion in 1958. Considering all of the problems that developed in 1958, it is extremely doubtful that the backlog position on preparatory work at the start of 1959 will be as good as it was at the start of 1958.

The cumulative record shows generally good results in eventually making planned sales. However, substantial parts of each year's sales program have been delayed. In 1955, 90,210 thousand boardfeet in planned sales were delayed, in 1956 the total delay volume dropped to 71,080 thousand board-feet, only to climb again in 1957 to 89,470 thousand board-feet, and drop to 43,000 thousand board-

feet in 1958. Additional sales made hardly balanced the program deferrals. The above tabulation understates the situation because it carries forward sales which appear in plans for 2 or more years. 

In addition, the record is often obscured because of variations between planned and actual volumes in sales. All planned figures are given on the announced plan basis, while sold figures reflect the actual volume offered.

However, taking the overall record, measuring backlog position and sales activity, it is evident that the sales program has not been in shape to fully respond to economic advances. 

The case history on this one forest makes it possible to now look at overall regional situations.

REGION 6, OREGON AND WASHINGTON 

In addition to the study of the Siskiyou National Forest the detailed review covered the Mount Hood, Gifford Pinchot, Snoqualmie, Okonagan, Willamette, and Umpqua National Forests. Other forest records were reviewed with regional office personnel. The annual allowable cut in this 18 forest region is 3.3469 billion board-feet. 

The region accounts for the largest part of the national forest timbersale business. The peak sales year was fiscal 1958 when sales amounted to 3.92 billion board-feet valued at $62.8 million. This was 49 percent of the national volume and 60 percent of the national value exclusive of the one large Alaska sale.

The peak cut year was 1957 when a volume of 2.75 billion boardfeet valued at $64.7 million was harvested. This was 43 percent of the total national-forest volume and 56 percent of the total income from timber sales.

This two-State region covers all the national forests in Oregon and Washington except for the Colville and part of the Kaniksu in the State of Washington which are in region 1. Region 6 timber sale business is not only large but its timber is exceedingly valuable. 

Although sales in this last fiscal year reached allowable cut levels the amount of timber cut by the industry is still only 82 percent of the allowable cut. There are, however, numerous working circles, most of which contain presently marketable timber which are not now and never have had sales made at or near permitted levels.¹ 

ANNOUNCED SALE PLANS 

Full information is available for the calendar years 1955, 1956, 1957, and for fiscal years 1955 through 1958. The calendar year data shows the relation between announced plans and accomplishments under them, while the fiscal year data shows the relation between financed programs and accomplishments.

SALES DELAY CAUSES 

The region was asked to supply a summary listing of reasons why planned sales were not made. The list furnished is not complete, but it does give a proportional picture of problems.

The region states that the prime cause for delay was inability to secure rights-of-way which affected over 1 billion board-feet in planned sales.

Delays in road construction and engineering was the second cause of delay affecting almost 900 million board-feet in planned sales.

Manpower problems constituted the third factor in importance,

affecting about one-half billion board-feet. The measure afforded by sales plans shows that the region did plan to and desired to sell more timber than it did.

Considerable time was expended in starting work on substantial volumes which could not be readied for sale. It is difficult to estimate the full effect that a firmer policy on rights-of-way might have on more promptly securing access to timber on reasonable terms and thus permitting its sale. Under the procedure that prevailed long, time-consuming negotiations were conducted. If these did not produce results additional time often was expended to locate a less advantageous and sometimes more costly route around the recalcitrant landowner, and the effort previously expended was lost. 

Delays in road construction and engineering were not segregated. 

Because in this region very little of the road construction is done with 

Federal funds, delays attributable to the Bureau of Public Roads are relatively few. Delays due to road construction occur because the timber purchaser does not complete the road on a prior sale by the date the Forest Service estimated it would be built. 

With the necessity of programing sales so as to best take advantage of roads to be built under timber sales, a slowdown in construction affects the sales program. More significant, however, has been the shortage of Forest Service engineering personnel to lay out proposed roads into planned sales. Region 6 handles annually over $30 million in timber purchase road construction, which is some 60 percent of the national total for the Forest Service, and involves some 1,800 miles of road. The average cost for these roads in the region in 1958 was $18,000 a mile. With construction costs of this order, it is essential that adequate engineering be provided. In addition, since road cost constitutes such a large and variable item of logging costs, it affects timber salability and valuations. Road location and its quality are important factors in determining the effectiveness of timber management as new areas are opened. The region has been under great pressure to meet timber management objectives, and road decisions are an important element in this goal. 

Manpower shortages, including those due to financing operation at less than announced sale plan levels, are the third ranking cause of sale delay. In view of the clear evidence that the region announced sales plans well above financed levels, it is not at all surprising to find one-half billion board-feet of unmade sales in this category. Delays occasioned by not completing road location also constitute a manpower shortage.

In summary, the announced plans were not related to timber sale funds provided or to jobload analysis data. The region precipitated the complaints it has had from industry by consistently announcing more sales than it was financed to handle. While the record of announced sales to actual sales looks poor, this is not true when financed sales are compared to actual sales. On the other hand, the announced sale program does represent the potential for sales that does exist and which should be financed.

Timber-to-be-cut estimates were scaled back to 2,995 million board-feet.
