3 In millions of board-feet.

During the past 4 fiscal years, although the total volume cut in each year has been virtually stable, the industry has cut a progressively lesser percentage of the region's financed base. 

There have been but few occasions when the industry has consistently cut as much timber as the 18 forests in region 6 were financed to handle. On 1 forest the financed cut was made in 3 of the 4 years; on 5 forests in 2 of the 4 years; on 5 other forests in only 1 of the 4 years, and on the balance, 7 forests, the cut never came up to financed levels.

The industry's cutting demands dropped over the last 4 years in relation to the Forest Service estimate of demand. In 1955 on 10 of the 18 forests the cut was in excess of financed levels. In 1956 this was true on only 5 forests; in 1957 the number dropped to 3, while in 

1958 the industry did not cut the financed volume from any forest in region 6.

The industry position really has been static on an overall basis with the same cut in 1955 and 1956, the cut in 1957, 5 percent over 1956, and the 1958 cut 6 percent under 1957. The net change, 1955 compared to 1958, was minus 1 percent.

This is somewhat surprising in view of the 30-percent increase in the volume of timber sold in 1957 as compared to 1955 and the 31percent increase in 1958 as compared to 1957. The sales level in 

1958 was 63 percent above the 1955 level. Thus, in addition to the fact that the region has operated at 95 percent of financed sale levels, there has been a marked advance over the last 4 years in total volume actually sold.

In 1955, 9 of the 18 forests sold amounts in excess of financed levels. 

In 1956 and 1957 the number dropped to 6, although actual volume sold advanced, while in 1958 it climbed to 10. At least once in the last 4 years every forest, except the Umatilla, has exceeded its financed sale goal. One forest did so all 4 years, 3 forests for 3 years, and 4 forests for 2 years.

The record for any one year is misleading. For example, over the 

4-year period, on the Ochoco and Okanagan Forests, where the Forest Service carries the full scaling load, each exceeded their financed sale program twice. Over the 4-year period the Ochoco sold only 90 percent of its financed level, while the Okanagan sold 111 percent. On the Ochoco sales dropped to 6 and 7 percent of financed levels in 1956 and 1957, then rose to 164 percent of the financed level in 1958. In one year the sales made were large, and work done in previous years showed up in sales accomplishments in the next year. However, during this period the Okanagan had a cut equal to 99 percent of its financed base, while the Ochoco cut dropped from the 99-percent level down to 48 percent of financed base. Certainly in this situation it would be reasonable to expect to find that the Ochoco's sale record would be better than it is. Both forests have leaned toward the less expensive to administer large sales and both forests have operated below levels planned by sales announcements. Current forest records do not permit a rapid assessment of the basic causes for variations in sales plans, either on the announced or financed level. In reality there need not be a close annual relation between timber cut and sold annually if it can be demonstrated that program announced plans have been met, planned sale preparation work accomplished, and both are geared to finances.

Meeting financed sell and cut goals is only part of the problem. 

More significant is how were the goals met. Among the factors that affect this are planned versus actual sale size, meeting time schedules and providing a reasonably constant flow of timber into the sale market. The concerns expressed by some industry spokesmen really reflects their reaction to these factors.

Deficiencies between announced plans and accomplishments are the only presently visible method of assessment. From an examination of the record it is clear that the forests and the region mistakenly announced plans far larger than they were financed to implement. 

This action was inexcusable because the industry did put reliance upon these announcements.

The fact is that over the last 4 fiscal years 9 forests have sold substantially more timber than their financed base provided; 2 more forests sold between 90 and 99 percent of their financed sale base; 

3 forests met between 80-89 percent of their financed sale base, while only 4 forests were below this figure. 

Three forests were most heavily criticized; the Gifford Pinchot which sold 87 percent of its financed base, the Mount Hood which sold 86 percent, and the Siskiyou which sold 82 percent of its base.

While this sale record was being made, these forests faced industry cutting demands well below those they were financed to handle. The 

Gifford Pinchot cut 82 percent of its financed base, the Mount Hood 

87 percent and the Siskiyou 73 percent. Under these circumstances it is not unreasonable to suspect that had cutting proceeded more nearly at financed levels, sales accomplishments would have been substantially below that which exists. 

CONTRACT ADMINISTRATION REQUIREMENTS AFFECT SALES PROGRAM 

The regional record does show that its estimates of the volume of timber that will be cut have been increasingly in error. On the other hand, the region appeared to be reasonably close in forecasting the financing that will be needed to make sales. To a large degree this performance benefit may result from the savings obtained by overestimating timber to be cut.

Under existing estimates the cost to administer sales in this region takes 80 percent of the timber sale dollar. However, the cut has averaged 12 percent below estimates. The theoretical result would be that 28 cents of each timber sale dollar became available for sale preparation, and it would follow that sales 40 percent in excess of financed levels would result. This did not occur. Instead sales averaged but 95 percent of financed levels. On the other hand, sales plans, while not geared to finances, called for selling about 40 percent more timber than was actually sold. The cost allowance for sale preparation and administration in this region understated the cost of doing business over the last 4 years.

Looking toward the future, the significant factor is that this region has substantially increased the volume of timber sold and this, in turn, is bound to increase cutting demands in the next few years. 

Since the cut phase of sale administration is four times as costly as preparation, there will be a substantial fall down in sales making unless appropriate revisions are made in future financial plans.

ESCALING PROVISION AFFECTS FUNDS 

Any review of timber sale problems in region 6 must recognize the unique timber measurement situation that exists only here. In 

January 1947 this region started to utilize private scaling bureaus to scale Forest Service timber. A little over half of the timber sold by log scale in the region now is measured by private bureaus. This step had the effect of decreasing the direct cost of doing business by reducing the use of appropriated funds for this contract administration service. Eight east side (pine) forests account for 25 percent of the timber scaled and the Forest Service still exclusively performs this operation. On the 10 west side forests, which account for the balance, the scaling is split 60 percent to the scaling bureaus and 40 percent to the Forest Service. Individual forest allotments for timber sales reflect the extent to which each type of scaling will be used. 

On a regional basis for calendar year 1956, the Forest Service scaled 7 percent more timber than in 1955, but in 1957 13 percent less timber was scaled by the Service than in 1956. On three forests where there was criticism of sale performance, the Siskiyou, Mount Hood, and Gifford Pinchot, the volume of timber scaled by the Service each year since 1955 has declined even more substantially and steadily. 

On the Siskiyou in 1957 there was a a 59 percent drop in timber scaled compared to 1955; on the Gifford Pinchot the drop was 36 percent, while on the Mount Hood it was 28 percent. There was not a commensurate increase in private bureau scaling. 

If on most forests the reduction in cut was caused by reduced daily operations, there would be no significant freeing of personnel by the change in cutting. It would be reasonable to expect that on the 

Mount Hood and Gifford Pinchot Forests sale performance was aided to some extent by the reduced cut. The large percentage drop in 

Service scaling on the Siskiyou is misleading, because it only involved a reduction from 33 million board-feet to 13.6 million board-feet, which are relatively small volumes. 

To the extent that private bureau scaling is contemplated, the financial base for the forest is reduced thus a forest where the volume to be handled by the scaling bureaus is increased, finds that its timber sale funds are reduced.

MANAGEMENT TOOLS TO IMPROVE PERFORMANCE 

The order of sales effort, general sales plans, detailed sale layout, and contract administration-with the timelag which necessarily exists between each-presents the Forest Service with an excellent means for better gearing its program to the economic needs of the area and to achieving budget forecasts which permit program fulfillment. Devices to do so have been until now largely unexplored and little used. 

Timber under contract

An example of a useful management tool heretofore little used is an analysis of the timber under contract. 

The region now compiles and publishes data which shows for each forest working circle the allowable cut, timber sold, and cut and the volume under contract at the start and close of the year and briefly

states the reason for undercuts in relation to allowable cuts.¹ This data reveals the progress in meeting overall management goals and shows quite fully those areas where timber sold, cut, and under contract is below desirable levels.

Many forest officers indicated as a rule of thumb that it was desirable to have as an absolute minimum twice the allowable cut under contract. This thesis, of course, is subject to verification and would vary with the size and length of contracts and local industry characteristics.

The region does not measure up to this rule of thumb and in fact only five forests meet or exceed it. In each case the timber under contract is held largely in one or two hands. Cutting patterns 

Better estimates of cutting plans can also be determined by analyzing the historic situation in various working circles and relating it to sale size and type of purchases.

Example of origin of timber cut on Willamette National Forest, calendar year 1957, by date of timber sale

16.3 32.2 27.8 76.3 21 42 37 100 20.4 4.8 1 89.0 27 139.8 42 80.7 24 334.7 100 There are significant variations between ranger districts in the rate at which timber sold is cut. On the Oakridge district only 2 percent of the timber cut in 1957 came from contracts sold that year. 

One third came from contracts over 4 years old. The Lowell district record showed purchasers drawing 70 percent of the timber cut in 1957 from contracts obtained the prior years, while on the Cascadia district only 42 percent of the timber cut came from the prior-year contracts, while 37 percent came from current-year contracts. On a forestwide basis the curve is smoother with the prior-year contracts being the major source of supply followed in order by 2-year old contracts and then the new contracts. This condition is largely a reflection of the delay occasioned in sales cutting created by the necessity for road construction prior to logging. On the other hand on one small district, not listed, 72 percent of its cut came from current sales and the balance from prior year sales. This is an area that is reasonably well roaded and favored by smaller purchasers. Local sales planning Since in the final analysis funds for sale preparation and administration are allotted to the ranger districts, and since it is here that the work is done, local knowledge of the conditions that prevail and are likely to develop, is essential to a smooth-running operation. While on the one hand the local personnel must develop the facts, on the other hand they need overall regional guidance and assistance to assure the sound program data is secured. 

A similar situation exists in sales planning. Local forest officers do prepare in this region detailed reports on the status of sales preparation. These reports are of restricted usefulness as management tools because they omit essential facts. It is presently impossible to make a really sound assessment of the actual volume of timber sales in the various stages of preparation at any given time. Current tabulations shows sales as a certain percentage prepared a tabulation which overlooks that it is not practical to bundle all the job aspects into a percentage and give it real meaning. One sale officer, on a forest with an outstanding sale record over the last 4 years, candidly stated that he had absolutely no confidence in these percentage figures.

He makes his own assessment of whether dead lines are going to be met. 

The importance of sale preparation work cannot be overemphasized. 

From the standpoint of the industry, it is actual sales that count. 

When timber is available for cutting the Forest Service, too, becomes more concerned. A priority demand for services is automatically created which cannot be dodged. From the record of the past 4 years, it is evident that this part of the job that takes 80 percent of the sales dollar is going to increase, and that current budget allocation will not be sufficient. If a downswing in sales offered is to be averted substantially, more knowledge will be needed on the goals and status of preparatory work. To accomplish this goal, sales planning revisions are needed. Presale elements of the job such as securing rights-of-way may have to be expressed in terms of cases time graded as to complexities. Road engineering may have to be expressed in terms of cost per mile. Local timetables which show total time allocated to each phase of the job and deadline dates would prove useful in assuring that local officers properly estimate the costs and complexities of the job and adhere to their estimates. On a regional basis inspections should be geared to determine whether plans are being met and the reasons for variations. To the extent that job loads are not adequately provided for in job analysis tables, it would be possible to make local adjustments at the regional level. In addition, there is a critical need to establish and adhere to dates for the completion of earnest negotiations for needed rights-of-way and then if agreement is not obtained, to promptly but fairly utilize the right of eminent domain to secure rights-of-way. Present indications are that there are substantial losses in efficiency because a firm right-of-way policy has not been developed and financed and implemented. While this study was not directed toward assessing the funds needed to solve rights-of-way purchase or road use agreements, there is no doubt but that they will be substantial. It is, however, an investment which will be fully recovered when the timber is sold. 

SUMMARY 

Region 6 has made greater advances on its own in developing techniques to gage its business than have the other regions studied. 

The current tempest about overall sale performance is largely centered in this region because of the error made in publicly announcing plans significantly in excess of financed levels. While large volumes of timber are theoretically available for cutting, the volume under contract is heavily concentrated in a few hands. Those who do not have timber under contract and were counting on announced sales to be offered, are understandably provoked at what they conceive to be a failure to produce. 

Measured against financed quotas the overall regional record on sales appears to be good but when measured against announced programs it is poor. The cutting record, which shows a lag behind sales reflects the sale makeup for the timber under contract. The region cannot safely appreciably expand its sales effort, unless additional funds and personnel are assured for contract administration, or until it better knows the cutting demands that could occur as a result of the recent upswing in sales. There is a good opportunity in this region to expand certain categories of the sales program such as

salvage sales. Further basic expansion will be largely controlled by road needs and the solution of rights-of-way problems. The records and data for the various elements of the sale program were not in a form which made it possible to make an analysis for the same date for each item. Thus, for example, it was not possible to accurately determine the total sale volume in various stages of preparation and relate it to goals.

REGION 5, CALIFORNIA 

The examination of this region which embraces California covered detailed examination of three forests-the Klamath, the Shasta- Trinity, and the Eldorado, as well as a general overall review of the region in the regional office. The annual allowable cut in region 5 is 

1.351 billion board-feet, which comes from 13 of the 17 national forests in the State. The other 4 forests in southern California are protection forests. The peak timber volume sold occurred in fiscal year 1956 when 1.178 billion board-feet, or 88 percent of the allowable cut, was marketed. Timber cut also reached its peak during that year, totaling 1.043 billion board-feet, 77 percent of the allowable cut.

FINANCED PROGRAM COMPARED TO PERFORMANCE 

This region has not made regular annual timber sales announcements, although currently their use is quite general in the region. 

Some of the announcements are on a calendar-year basis, while others are on the fiscal year. Because sale plan use has been spotty and they are not all on a similar time period, an analysis of sale accomplishments in relation to announced plans would be incomplete. Where announced plans were utilized, the same general situation prevailed as was found in region 6-the timber offered for sale was substantially below announced volumes and the announced volumes were well above financed quotas.

1 Angeles, Los Padres, San Bernardino, Cleveland not under regulated cut.

The region 5 record shows a performance less than satisfactory in some aspects. Evaluation of this record must take into account the fact that present timber-sale activity on the forests in region 5 is far above the 1950 level and, in addition, these forests have a higher percentage of species not sought by the industry than do the forests in the Pacific Northwest. Timber sold has generally risen but fluctuated unevenly, and during the last 2 years there has been both a substantial drop in the real volume sold as well as in the percentage of the financed sales that the region has offered. The regional average sales for the last 4 fiscal years have been 75 percent of financed levels set by the region, but in the last 2 years there was a significant drop to 50 and 69 percent of these financed levels. Of the 13 forests in the last 4 years only on 16 occasions have forests met their regional financed sales level. One forest-the Klamath-met or exceeded goals in 3 of the 4 years, 4 others met them in 2 years, 5 forests in only 1 year, and 3 have never reached sales goals. The record shows 

5 forests, however, have met or exceeded sales goals on a 4-year average basis.

The number of forests meeting sales goals has dropped from 5 in 1955 and 1956, respectively, to 3 in 1957 and 1958. 

Timber-cutting demands by the industry show that in 1955 and 1956, 9 and 10 forests, respectively, met or exceeded financed cutting goals, while in 1957 and 1958 the number dropped to 3 and 2, respectively. At least once in the last 4 years the cut on each forest exceeded financed goals, and on 4 forests this has occurred in 3 years, while in 2 others it occurred twice.

The record shows that on an overall basis the industry cut 90 percent of the timber the region was supposedly financed to handle, but only on 2 forests did the cut exceed finances for the 4-year period. 

In 1955 and 1956, goals were met or exceeded while in 1957 and 1958 the cut dropped to 78 percent of financed levels. While, on the one hand, the overall cutting record on the various forests shows relatively little variation between forest, on the other hand, the sales record varies widely. Even during the 2 years when the region generally exceeded sales goals, certain forests with overall poor records failed to meet their quota goals.

An earlier table (p. 42) showed the general drop in timber sold and cut in 1957-58 as compared to 1956 and also showed that sales climbed in 1958 while cut dropped as compared to 1956. The above table shows a 263 million board-feet increase in timber under contract between June 30, 1957 and 1958. Only 7 forests increased the volume under contract and the gains were largely concentrated upon 2 of the forests. The bulk of the gain came from increased sales and the balance from a decrease in timber cut in 1958. The unreconciled difference is due to better estimates of the uncut volume remaining on contracts. On some forests, such as Klamath, the volume cut in 1957 and 1958 did not continue to increase despite the generally good sale record and the excellent record in 1955 and 1956. The result was that increased sales were mainly reflected in an increase in timber under contract.

The general rule of thumb that forest officers gave was that to have a smoothly functioning sale program it was desirable as an absolute minimum to have the equivalent of twice the annual allowable cut under contract. However, only 1 forest, the Mendocino, has attained this criteria and 6 of the forests have about 50 percent or less than this amount under contract. In the case of the Mendocino, the goal was only realized in 1958, and in fact the gains achieved by the other forests are due primarily to the sales effort in 1958. The 

Mendocino forest has had a very erratic sale record and only in 1958 did the cut begin to rise. To the extent that sales on which cutting will be deferred for several years make up the backlog, they do not contribute to the goal of having sufficient timber under contract to meet the bulk of cutting expectations for the next 2 years. 

Region 5 is in a critical position both in terms of the volume under contract and its advance sale preparation work. This situation stems to a large degree from the explosive growth in the timber sale business which has not been accompanied by timely investments in a basic road system, up-to-date management plans, personnel housing, and adequate personnel to develop a backlog of prepared sales. 

GROWTH OF BUSINESS, KLAMATH FOREST 

On the Klamath Forest, for example, saleswork now is virtually on a day-to-day basis with practically no advance work in process. This forest made a tremendous sale effort in 1956 following the Haystack fire. It enjoyed good success not only in meeting overall program goals, having sold 102 percent and cut 101 percent of financed quotas over the last 4 years, but also in actually selling more timber than in announced plans. This record has been at the expense of developing an adequate backlog of presale preparation work. 

Illustrative of the growth problems this forest has faced is this analysis of the last 9 years.

Timber cut and sold, Klamath National Forest, region 5 471

In 9 years there has been a 2,100-percent increase in timber cut and a 2,180-percent rise in timber sold. The climb is clear evidence that in 1950 this forest virtually did not have a timber-sale business. 

Timber cut and sold has shown not only a general growth pattern, but a very irregular growth. Under these circumstances, it is not difficult to see that there have been extensive problems in making the transition from custodial operation to management. Over the 9-year period, the total volume sold exceeds the volume cut by 190 million board-feet. By the end of 1958, this forest had 224 million boardfeet under contract. During the last 4 years the timber cut on this forest has averaged only 77 percent of the allowable cut, while sales have averaged 96 percent.

PROGRAM AND FUND COORDINATION, ELDORADO FOREST 

Each forest has a general plan setting forth funds needed and program goals which is submitted to the region to aid in budget preparation and to secure fund allocation. For example, in preparing for the 1958 budget the Eldorado Forest indicated that 69 million board-feet would be cut and that it would sell 122 million board-feet. 

The subsequent timber business report was on the same basis and a fund allocation of $61,500 was sought for timber sales. The allotment from the region was $47,700 and the cut-and-sell goal was still required by the region despite the 23-percent fund reduction. The amount of timber actually cut was only 42 million board-feet, or 61 percent of the quota. Timber sold amounted to 67 million feet, or 

55 percent of the quota. If unit cost factors for sales accurately reflected the actual cost, the allotment necessary to do the job would have been but $36,800, or $10,800 less than actually allotted. While no separate tabulation was made by fiscal years, 32 million boardfeet sold in fiscal year 1958 was a carryover of sales planned for earlier years.

Indications are that unit costs for timber sales preparation and administration were insufficient to meet needs. A significant need on the Eldorado was to develop concrete solutions to several large problems which govern the effectiveness of the entire sale program. 

This is a forest which apparently has had numerous longstanding problems in meeting sale goals. Securing overall rights-of-way agreements has been a significant factor in holding up sales planning. The forest also has been in the process of shifting over to 100 percent premarking before advertisement. In addition, several sales have had to be replanned to render them salable and there have been a series of difficulties in finding interested bidders for planned sales. This last situation is one which is markedly different from region 6. On numerous occasions the region's record would show that sales were not offered because a prospective customer indicated a lack of interest in purchasing timber and apparently a number of forests have an informal policy of not offering timber if a regular customer requests the sale not be offered. In sales with heavy white fir stands involving extensive road construction, the number of bidders is automatically reduced. To offer the timber when the only firm that could bid indicates it would not, generally would result in no bid being received. 

In some instances when the sale was later offered, however, the firm that requested the delay was not the successful bidder. Sale programing in these situations would be aided by advance road construction with appropriated funds which would enable the region to vary sale size and species composition to meet a greater variety of potential customers.

In region 5 timber management is complicated by the other important multiple-use considerations. Watershed protection and recreational use are high-priority programs because of California's chronic water problems and expanding population. In addition, there are serious shortages in the basic road network and rights-of-way, so that if sales cannot be distributed evenly over each working circle, planning in excess of regular needs is required. It is not feasible to concentrate sales in lower accessible areas, which, while thinly justifiable as keeping the cut up to, but not above, sustained-yield levels, cuts off the face of the working circle. Already this region is being caught more and more between the jaws of a vise with concentrated pressure on one side to sell more timber and on the other side with general pressure to more fully consider other multiple-use aspects.

The extent to which additional sales can be programed based on existing roads is limited. The following tabulation lists some of the areas where roads are needed to attain the allowable cut. These forests are also the ones which generally have a sales record well below the regional average.

SUMMARY 

While the California region has many of the same problems as the Pacific Northwest there are additives which increase its complexity. 

Watershed protection is more complex. Recreation is an increasing and a demanding use. Timber stands are generally lighter per acre and have a high percentage of species which are often difficult to market. This contributes to the problem of financing main-line road construction through timber sales and affects marketability of sales. 

The long-term solution of timber management problems in this region also will require meeting the challenge posed by complex multiple-use relationships.

During the recent peak stumpage price period in 1955 when many Douglas-fir region timber operators were bidding on national-forest timber in a way that made timber appraisals look like bargain basement prices, California timber purchasers were objecting to appraised rates for national-forest timbers while also seeking to have more timber offered for sale. All indications are that accelerated development of the basic road system would permit the Forest Service to achieve sale flexibility. This would help tremendously to increase the volume of timber that could be sold and permit making sales more attractive to the industry.

The sale record in this region shows that the cut is lagging behind sales and that both are substantially below possible levels. Extremely small amounts of timber are in process of preparation for sale. The volume of timber under contract is below optimum levels. There are real opportunities to provide some quick aid in many areas by increasing the volume of salvage timber sold in roaded areas. 

REGION 1, NORTHERN REGION 

This region encompasses 16 national forests located primarily in 

Montana and northern Idaho, but also in Washington and South

Dakota. Field study was made on the Lolo, Flathead, and Kootenai 

Forests in Montana and the Kaniksu in Idaho, and a detailed forestby-forest review was conducted on the balance of the forest at the regional office.

Just prior to this inspection, region 1 had started its own review of sales directed toward ascertaining the reasons why sales were not measuring up to program levels. This extensive forest-by-forest tabulation was speeded up to provide the committee with the basic information in the general form sought in time for the field review. 

This 16-forest region has an allowable cut of 1.453 billion boardfeet, but only 1.039 is saw timber over 11 inches in diameter breast high. In this review the latter figure has been used because it more nearly represents the gross volume that is currently salable. Even at that, an additional 84 million board-feet of saw timber on four forests (the Beaverhead, Custer, Deerlodge, and Helena) has been of marginal salability.

It also contains a substantial but unevaluated volume resulting from differences between the volume in planned sales and actual advertised volumes. 

4 Lack of market would probably have prevented the sale of most of this volume even if right-of-way problems had been solved.

The purpose of the analysis is to summarize the major reasons for failure to make proposed sales, and to show the 3 calendar year record related to sawtimber allowable cut. 

Proposed timber sales in this region totaled 2,788.1 million boardfeet, or 88 percent of the sawtimber allowable cut, but only 1,365.1 million board-feet, or 49 percent of this volume was offered for sale. 

The 51 percent balance constitutes 1,422.5 million board-feet in sales not advertised, including a reconciliation of the difference between proposed and actual volumes in sales made. There was 95.5 million board-feet advertised but not sold. The region sold additional unplanned volumes to bring actual offerings during the period to 2,299.9 million board-feet or 74 percent of the allowable cut. 

The tabulation shows quite concisely the major sources of difficulties in meeting sales goals. Unsettled rights-of-way or road cost-sharing agreements leads the list of problems accounting for 528.4 million board-feet-or about half of the timber not sold. 

About 20 percent of the volume listed in this group was on the Beaverhead Forest, where marketing problems assumed larger proportions than the right-of-way factor during the negotiation period. 

Another 25 percent is on the Kootenai Forest often involving tripartite land ownership between the Forest Service, the Northern Pacific

Railroad and J. Neils Division of the St. Regis Paper Co. In this region there are firms who have an extensive checkerboard ownership with the Forest Service. The development of long-term road development, use and cost sharing agreements is time consuming. While negotiations are proceeding amicably, they are not proceeding rapidly enough to permit offering planned sales.

The second ranking cause for sale deficiencies is lack of proper market involving 253.9 million board-feet. This group contains two problems, both sometimes closely related. Unroaded stands with large quantities of low value timber present marketing difficulties and this region has a substantial forest area in this condition. There are areas with substantial lodgepole pine which are difficult to market in quantity. In other cases there are few properly capitalized mills near forest areas. Thus, sales depend upon prior road construction to bring them within the investment range these smaller mills can consider. As long as there are alternate sources of supply, low value inaccessible national forest timber will prove difficult to move. 

Third in order of importance was lack of personnel, due in part to financing salework at less than announced levels, was limited to four forests with the situation apparently quite aggravated on the Clearwater Forest. This is a forest where there has been substantial variations in the volume of timber cut in various years as well as in different quarters of each year. Based on the overall regional record, it is interesting that lack of personnel is so small a factor. In reality, considering total accomplishments, it is extremely doubtful that the existing level of financing would have permitted work to be completed on the 528.4 million board-feet that were not sold due to right-of-way problems. "Other" causes include 125.7 million board-feet in sales deferred because of the dark beetle program. It also includes the reconciliation of a 260 million board-feet difference between planned sales volume and actual volume, and therefore is not a real measure of loss due to failure to offer the number of sales planned. This "lost" volume does present a problem because unless good presale

estimates are made, financed levels of operation will not be met unless additional sales are included in the program. 

Volumes advertised but not sold represented only 7 percent of the sales actually advertised, a relatively small volume but on the Clearwater Forest it was a substantial part of the advertised volume.

The tables show both the sold and cut record for 3 fiscal years-1956-58. In addition, the region supplied a tabulation showing the announced program. The somewhat "better" fiscal year sales record comes in part from the difference between fiscal and calendar years and some additional reappraisal volumes under the bark beetle program shows in the calendar year table which are not shown in the fiscal year table due to reporting differences..

The 16 forests in this region have sold their financed program only 7 times during the last 3 fiscal years. On a 3-year basis only the 

Lewis and Clark met or exceeded the financed level. The Bitterroot which was the only forest to exceed its financed level during the period in more than 1 year, sold 97 percent of its financed program. Six forests exceeded their financed sale program in 1 year, but 10 forests never met or exceeded financed goals.

The industry cut timber in excess of the forests financed level 14 times in the period and on 2 forests, the Coeur d'Alene and Colville, the 3-year average was in excess of financed levels. In 1956 the cut on 9 forests exceeded financed levels, but this dropped off to 3 and 2 forests, respectively, in 1957 and 1958. On 11 forests at least once in the period cut exceeded financed levels with this occuring in 2 or more years on 3 forests, and only in 1 year on the other 8 forests. 

Five forests did not have cutting demands equal to financed levels in any of the 3 years and they also never sold their financed program. 

The total volume cut in region 1 is 412 million board-feet in excess of the volume sold during the same period; the reverse of the situation prevailing elsewhere. In contrast with region 5, however, the percentage of financed timber sold improved in 1957 and 1958 över 1956. In common with region 5 the percentage of timber cut to financed base was better than the sold record, and it showed the same sort of falloff over the period.

The major factor for concern is that during this 3-year period, 412 million board-feet more timber was actually cut than was sold and the region's position in timber under contract compared to 1955 had not improved. In 1956 and 1957, cut exceeded sales by 538 million board-feet which represent a tapering off of the bark beetle control and salvage logging program. The 1958 sales record showed a 220 million board-feet increase in the volume sold over 1957, and a 140 million board-feet decrease in volume cut which started to reverse the prior years trend although this did not overcome the results of those years.

TIMBER SALE PREPARATION 

The region prepared a summary analysis of the current position of timber sales in process of preparation, but records were not readily available on prior years. However, taken together with the change in the cut and sold position, the indications are that while the timber under contract has decreased, the volume partially prepared to sell has risen.

The following table shows the estimated sale preparation backlog as of June 30, 1958:

This 1.582 billion feet of carryover from fiscal year 1958, for the most part, represents cruising work and initial road reconnaissance or some other fieldwork. 

For most of this volume considerably more fieldwork (securing of rights-of-way, development of logging plans, survey and design of roads, development of costsharing agreements) and office work is needed to complete sale preparation. 

The picture for the region shows that a relatively large amount of the timber to be sold in a subsequent year has had some work done on it at least a year prior to the sale. Yet the sale record shows a low level of accomplishment in relation to a planned program and that a relatively sizable volume is sold in additional unplanned sales. The type of work completed in advance of sale is not the most timeconsuming aspect and the regions record coupled with its estimate of its situation indicates that substantial improvement is needed in its advance sale preparation to achieve a position where planned sale goals can more readily be met.

TIMBER UNDER CONTRACT 

The other element of the sale program which is summarized is timber under contract as of July 1, 1958: 

Total uncut volume under contract_ (b) Contract volume available for cutting this fiscal year. (c) Contract volume not available for cutting in fiscal year 1959_ Million board-feet 1,823 1, 548 275 The volume shown as available for cutting this fiscal year is the total volume of timber under contract not cut and not restricted from cutting this fiscal year. 

The volumes not available for cutting this fiscal year represent long-term timber sales. While short-term (1 to 3 years) contracts do not usually limit rate of cutting, logging plans, and practical application of logging methods dictate that the available volume will be spread over 2 and, in some cases, 3 or more years.

The real volume available for cutting in fiscal year 1959 is 1.548 billion board-feet, about 1½ times allowable cut and about twice the average annual cut. The distribution among customers of the timber under contract determines the real availability of the timber to regional mills. Customer preferences vary and some desire to have substantial volumes under contract while others prefer to operate with a small inventory. This must be considered in decisions on the total volume in relation to allowable cut that should be under contract. 

In addition, the amount of road construction that the timber purchaser must accomplish before he can commence to log will also affect the real annual availability of timber under contract. 

In the last 3 years sales have averaged 740 million board-feet a year and cut 840 million board-feet a year indicative of a decrease in timber under contract. The region also reports only 15 percent of the inventory is composed contract volumes which could not be cut this year due to restrictions. Under current marketing practices, which require extensive roadbuilding as a part of a timber sale, either a substantially larger volume would have to be under contract to permit removal of the 1,039 million board-feet annual allowable cut, or else a tremendous shift to sales not requiring roads would be required.

EFFECT OF VARIATIONS BETWEEN PLANNED AND ACTUAL SALE SIZE 

On all of the forests in all regions, there were significant variations between planned sale sizes estimated in the timber business report when funds were requested and the actual size of sales made. There are often good reasons why actual sales will vary from plans, but the variations should lead to revision of expected output inasmuch as sales funds are budgeted and allocated largely on forecasts of sale size. Plans flow up to the Chief's office, and after Congress provides the funds the flow back to the forest level is usually closely proportional to estimates. On the forest the funds do not retain an identity which relates work done to sale sizes planned. 

Because records are not kept on a reportable basis, the tables below are based on correlated standard cost allocations for planned and actual work. As part of its timber management funds the forest did receive a $15,800 allocation for the planned sale preparation totaling 112.5 million board-feet. For purposes of describing the problem, it is assumed that, based upon sales actually made, totaling 82 percent of planned volume, 92.3 million board-feet in the originally planned sizes were processed; that costs were as estimated, and the job should have been done for $12,942. Based upon the sale sizes that were actually made, costs estimated at $13,551 were incurred. This is 10.47 percent, or $609, more than estimated costs. If the total volume had been processed, if the forest had shifted sale size as was done in this case, and if cost estimates were correct, this forest would have needed $16,543, or $743, more than its allotment.

The Service does not utilize a cost-accounting system which reveals the actual funds expended upon each sale by size class; therefore, it is impossible to determine just what the exact cost was for sellling this 92.3 million board-feet. Nor is it known what portion of the $15,800 was spent on presale work which will not mature until next year, or what portion was used to finance the administration of cutting, which in that year was 104 percent of the financed base. The problem is that a shift in sale size to the more or less costly sale can measurably affect the performance record of a forest. 

In this example the forest sold only 65 percent of the volume it planned in very small sales and 71 percent of the volume planned in larger sales. In the process it sold 30.9 million board-feet in a sale size class larger than was planned. The biggest shift was in the $2,0002-million-board-foot class where sales were 430 percent above plans. 

With accurate costs for doing business, estimates on timber cut proceeding at planned rates, a shift which raises the cost of sales work by 

10 percent would have serious consequences. 33630 O-59-5

The lack of correlation between plans and performance is a financial management problem which extends to all facets of the sale program from presale work through scaling. The tabulations also show that while 12.9 percent of the volume was planned for sale in the 2 smallest sales classes, actually only 10.3 percent was sold in these classes. 

Based on plans, one-third of sales funds would have been allocated to 

12.9 percent of the timber to be sold. Based on actual 24.1 percent of the volume sold in these classes, it is estimated that they required 

48.1 percent of the sale-preparation funds.

The forest selected is one where very small sales are about double the regional average. The regional program for 1958 planned sales as listed below. The actual sales by size class are not presently included in the timber business report and are not shown.

Average sale cost, both for the regional and the Flathead example is the 2-million-board-foot to 5-million-board-foot sale size. In the regional example, the 19.1 percent of sales smaller than this were estimated to require 37.4 percent of the sales dollar, while the 57.2 percent in the large sales classes is estimated to require 10.7 percent of the sales dollar. The average class, with 23.7 percent of sales volume, requires 24.1 percent of the sales funds.

A relatively small increase in volume planned in smaller sales can raise costs while not measurably increasing total volume sold, while a small shift of funds to the larger sales should bring about a substantial increase in volume sold.

Actual sales costs in relation to income are so low that sales sizes decisions should be made on overall policy objectives rather than costs. 

Nevertheless, variations from planned sale size do affect performance with the funds allocated. Thus, there should be some basic but uncomplicated control on performance. 

The relative costs for the various sale sizes indicates that consideration might be given for cost purposes to a breakdown of the group accounting for the most volume-the 5-million-board-foot to 15-million-board-foot size class-into 2 groups and to combining the 2 largest groups, 15 million board-feet to 25 million board-feet and over 25 million board-feet into 1 group. Another situation which was examined was that while a sale may be in the 5-million-board-foot to 15-million-board-foot group when sold, the rate of cut may be equal to a 2-million-board-foot to 5-million-board-foot sale. The Service

is currently considering reporting these sales as smaller ones for cost estimate purposes, an improvement that will help better reflect the true cost of administration. 

SALVAGE SALES OPPORTUNITIES EXIST 

There are small sales opportunities amounting to about 150 million board-feet a year in the present roaded areas where timber can be salvaged. Currently, this region is financed to handle about one-fifth of this potential. A special review on the Swan Lake district of the 

Flathead National Forest showed that between January 1 and October 22, 1958, this district expended $2.14 per thousand board-feet to make 27 salvage sales totaling 1,777,000 board-feet and averaging 66,000 board-feet in size and an income of $800 per sale. The gross income from these sales was $12.10 per thousand board-feet, or over $5 for each dollar expended. Under current conditions and with its existing road network, the Flathead Forest estimates it could raise its present sale volume from 6 million to 20 million board-feet. 

This was the only forest on which special data was accumulated, but it is estimated by the region that total potential is 150 million boardfeet. At current prices, additional gross income of $1,800,000 would be realized for an expenditure of about $350,000. An important aspect of these salvage programs beyond the good sale revenue potential is that the timber cut is outside the allowable cut and thus not chargeable to it. This is material which is the normal mortality loss under current standards of management. Its harvest serves a useful economic purpose by increasing the amount of timber that can be cut without jeopardy to sustained yield. The opportunity for a sale program of this type will, of course, diminish over the years as oldgrowth stands are converted to younger managed stands, but for the present this is a good means of aiding forest industry make adjustments to sustained-yield cutting levels. 

SUMMARY 

Region 1 has a galaxy of distinct problems with its large areas in lodgepole pine, the attendant problem of selling material of less than saw-log size, the need to develop a pulp industry, and stark contrasts between areas with a well-developed forest-products industry and undeveloped areas. Its sale performance must be considered against this background. The need for road construction and rights-of-way in this region, as in other regions, does not show up fully in directly measurable quantities. Where no roads exist, large-scale development sales are utilized or else no sales are planned. These large sales are generally not put into programs until a major local operator shows an interest. Similarly, where rights-of-way are not available, sales are often not planned. It is significant that planned sales generally represent situations where long-existing problems appear on the verge of solution

To have a fully effective sale program, the region needs to increase the volume of timber in process for sales and to increase the volume of timber under contract. The quickest immediate increase can be obtained by salvage sales, but a sustained increase will require basic development investments.

INTRODUCTION 

The Okanogan National Forest, comprised of 2,041,645cres (actually owned inside the boundary), is located in the north central part of the State of Washington. It is part of what was originally the 

Washington Forest Reserve established by proclamation of President 

Cleveland on February 22, 1897, and was established as a National 

Forest by proclamation of President Theodore Roosevelt on July 1, 1908. It occupies portions of Okanogan and Chelan Counties, and extends from the Canadian Border on the north to the Chelan-Entiat Divide on the south, and from the Cascade Summit on the west to the Okanogan County line on the east. The principal drainages are Lake 

Chelan, Methow River, Salmon Creek, San Poil River, Kettle River and their tributaries. The entire forest is in the ponderosa pine subregion.

Elevations range from 410 feet below sea level (bottom of Lake Chelan) to 9,500 feet at the summit of Bonanza Peak. Topography is characteristically rugged and broken, with steep, narrow canyons and high intervening ridges, but there are within the forest some 900,000 acres suitable for tractor logging. Soil types are variable and characteristically quite shallow with frequent outcroppings and loose boulders. Most of the area is of volcanic origin with a light volcanic ash soil which is susceptible to erosion damage. Because the forest is located east of the Cascade Divide, precipitation is generally light, varying from 9 to 40 inches annually. The climate is moderate, but varies in range from a minimum of 400 below zero to a maximum of 120°above. Field and logging seasons are from five months to year around, varying with elevation. Fire season is generally confined to the period from June through September. 

Use of resources dates back to 1895 when large herds of livestock grazed in the Loomis Country. The first records of timber sales were in 1909. Records also show that permits were issued as early as 1910 for recreational purposes such as summer residences and resorts. Water from the forest has always held a primary position of importance. The Okanogan National Forest furnishes timber, water, forage, fish and wildlife and recreation, each in sufficient amounts to be commercially important. Early history records the occurrence of large fires which have diminished in both size and number under organized protection. Sale of timber products has been rather ambstantial since 1936, and as on most other forests, increased greatly during World War II. On the following pages are tabulations showing acreage burned and timber harvested by years. The summary of burned acreage for the period 1907 to 1941 inclusive does not include the burned acreage for the Tonasket Ranger District since that area did not become a part of the Okanogan National Forest until 1942. 

1/ Includes the Lake Chelan District which is within the established forest boundary but under the administration of the

SOCIAL AND ECONOMIC RELATIONSHIPS 

The Okanogan National Forest is in many respects important from social and economic standpoints. The population in the immediate zone of influence is small (39,000 to 45,000 variance due to seasonal influx), but the general dependence of the communities upon the forest resources is substantial. Water, recreation, grazing, fish, wildlife, timber, minerals all from the forest, play an important role in the everyday welfare of the population. 

The forest is reached from the south and north by U.S. Highway #97. 

State Highway #16 serves the. Methow Valley. State Highway #10 and #10A carry traffic from the Spokane area to the forest. The 

Great Northern Railroad runs from Wenatchee to Canada. In addition to the state and federal highways in the area, there is a network of approximately 1,280 miles of forest and county roads which make much of the forest accessible to vehicles. The closest commercial airline transportation is at Wenatchee.

There is considerable diversification of industry. The following tabulation shows the primary means of livelihood for those gainfully employed:

Industry 

Percent of

Employment Mining Orchards 12.5 40.0 

Forest Industries.

13.0 100.0 

The manufacture of forest products plays an important role in the economic well-being of the entire area and is of real importance to some of the smaller outlying communities. The log requirement map on the following page shows annual log needs of various mills within the zone of influence of the Okanogan National Forest. 

Competition for federal stumpage among operators is growing. Sales on this forest have been awarded to firms 125 miles distant at bid prices exceeding appraised prices. The actual social and economic relationship of the timber resource is further discussed under "Community Stability" but here it is sufficient to say that some 100 million board feet of government and private timber is cut annually with an estimated lumber retail value of $8,000,000.00. -11-

Water is one of life's basic needs, and most of the 30,000 people in Okanogan County are dependent upon the forest for water for all uses. Aside from the domestic supply, the following shows the dependency upon water from the forest for irrigation!

Industry Orchards Hay

Pasture Totals

2,240,000 lbs. beef grain 4.7 25,155 38.4 

Irrigated acreage only

Dairying, fresh produce and many other phases of farming would raise this total to better than $40,000,000.00. 

Recreation use has long been recognized as big business by the people in and adjoining the forest. Plans and developments for taking advantage of the recreation potentials of the forest have gone ahead with some of the other industries. The good · hunting and fishing that is found throughout most of the forest draws people from all over the state, and the scenic beauty draws tourists from all parts of the United States. The annual combined value of recreation, hunting and fishing to the community is estimated at over $2,500,000.00.

Grazing has always held a high position on the Okanogan. During the past ten years grazing has averaged some 102,700 animal months annually, with a high of 142,000 in 1945 and a low of 64,000 in 1950. The trend seems to be toward a more or less stabilized 

70,000 animal months annually. Some 24,000 sheep and cattle graze on National Forest Land. The yearly income from these animals, with calves and lambs, is estimated to be more than $4,000,000.00. 

There is an abundance of fur-bearing animals on the forest, but only a very small percentage of the population engage in trapping, and then only as a part tino occupation or as a winter pastime. 

Yearly income from this fiela is estimated at $10,000.00. 

There are on the forest some 126,000 acres of mineralized zones. 

(Reference Memo, Chelan, U-LEGISLATION, Federal, March 28, 1952.) 

-13-

Yuch unproductive prospecting has been done in many of these areas and to some extent prospecting is still active. Mining employs only a small part of the total population, but the value of one mine has proven quite sizeable. The Howe-Sound Mine at Holden supports the town of Holden with a population of over 600 and is the largest mining operation of the forest. The town is one of the most unique special uses in the Forest Service. The annual income from this operation is estimated to be $2,800,000.00. 

Copper, gold and zinc are the metals mined here in that order of importance. Other mines on the forest show some promise, but to date are of no consequence.

The above is sufficient to clearly show that from the social and economic standpoint, the importance of the Okanogan National Fores should not be minimized nor its potentials underestimated.

SALES POLICY 

The sales policy for the Okanogan forest is outlined in the points listed below: 

a. Size of Sales 

Size of sales will be governed by the needs of industry, forest development, and the forest stands. Considering the first, there are only four mills in the area producing 10 MMBF or nore annually. These four concerns account for about 

70% of the total payroll derived from logging and milling in this area. To promote community stability, it is judged desirable to place offerings on the market which will permit these firms to produce continuously if they can compete successfully. There are some 22 other logging and/or milling concerns in or adjacent to the forest who individually have annual log requirements of 5 MMBF or less. It will be the policy to place as many small offerings (5 MMBF or less) on the market as is possible consistent with other objectives. 

Considering forest development, the Okanogan has only about 

20% of the eventual planned transportation system. As has been pointed out in other places in this plan, the average volume per acre is low and road construction is difficult. 

Therefore, it is necessary to make large sales to finance development in some drainages. This is a factor which must be considered when preparing action plans. 

The silvicultural need for cutting and size of natural logging units are practical considerations in determining the size of any specific offering. 

b. Point of Manufacture 

The local communities stand to gain materially when sales of sawlogs are manufactured into lumber or more finished products in local plants. However, there is no provision of any kind requiring local manufacture. 

ci Logging Methods 

Most of the logging done on this forest is accomplished by tractors. The Wyssen system is being tried and may revolutionize procedures. As more clear-cutting is done, high lead equipment may be tried. Some horse-logging may be expected when intermediate cuts become more frequent. There is presently no policy prohibiting any particular type of equipment. Critical sites, from the standpoint of soil movement, have been excluded from sale areas.

DESCRIPTION OF BLOCK 

This unit lies between the Okanogan River and the Okanogan-Ferry County line and extends from the Colville Indian Reservation Boundary on the south, north to the Canadian Boundary. It embraces all of the Tonasket Ranger District and is entirely within Okanogan County. The district is comprised of five units of national forest land with intervening lands of various ownerships. There are 

228,633 acres within the proclamation boundary of the unit with ownership divided approximately as follows:

Ownership Class U.S. Forest Service 

Private

State of Washington Percent of Area 

94.5 4.2 1.3 100.0

Historically this block has had a comparatively long period of activity from the standpoint of timber harvest. A rather substantial volume has been cut annually since around 1936. At present this unit ranks second on the forest in timber sale activity. 

The following is a tabulation of the volume cut during the past years: Period

FOREST DESCRIPTION 

This unit is characterized by a rolling type of topography and generally is not as steep and rocky as is the largest percentage of the forest. Being on the extreme eastern portion of the forest it les in the lower foothills of the Cascades. The timber is -باو-

composed mainly of old and second-growth Ponderosa pine, Douglasfir, and Western larch, roughly divided into two zones. 

The pine zone is situated south of an imaginary line drawn from northwest to southeast across the district, the fir-larch types lying north of this line. There are very few pure Ponderosa pine stands, this species generally occurring in varying mixtures with fir and larch. 

It is foundranging in elevation from 2,000 to 4,000 feet in the well drained areas and is not found as abundantly in the lower valleys and streams as is typical on most of the forest. Instead, large stands of Western larch and Douglas-fir are found here with occasional smell stands of Engelmann spruce in mixture on larger stream bottoms.

The fir-larch type is composed of a mixture of these two species with some areas of pure fir and pure larch interspersed. The fir is of very inferior quality by comparison with westside stands and the larch is about equal in quality to the fir. Both of these species are particularly susceptible to windshake and witches broom. 

Lodgepole pine is found generally above 4,000 feet although it is occasionally found at lower elevations in mixture with the firlarch type.

Defect in old growth of all species is estimated to average about 8%. The average regulated commercial stand per acre throughout the unit is approximately 6,400 board feet. Young stands are generally well stocked. The relative proportions of occurrence of generalized forest types and volumes in given in the appendix along with more detailed resource summaries, OBJECTIVES, PROBLEMS AND PRESCRIPTIONS 

Aside from the generalized aims of national forest timber management as described in the forest plan and elsewhere, specific aims on this unit are as follows: lar area,

A. Deve Lop through timber harvest the Aeneas Creek firthe Bench Creek pine area and the Bonaparte 

Mt.. Lodgepole area.

B. Pay particular attention to the problems of coordination of timber management and other forms of land use, specifically recreation, forage and water production. 

1. Timber Access Roads: 

At the present time 298 miles of access roads are proposed for construction or reconstruction to fully develop the unit. In addition to the construction of new roads, the possibilities of reconstructing specific segments of existing roads should be reviewed. -95-

in conjunction with each sale made. No federal access roads are presently proposed on this unit, but the possibility should be considered in those areas where it is planned that the timber will be removed by small operators and the volume removed by any one operator will not be sufficient to pay for the road. Rights-ofway will have to be secured on many of the proposed roads but no difficulty is anticipated. 

2. Coordination With Other Uses: 

8.

Recreation 

No dedicated areas exist on this block, but there are several small multiple use reservations on which recrcation is the primary use. A more detailed description of these reservations is given in the appendix of this plan.

Since complete harvest cutting is not contemplated in these areas the resource contained is not included in the computation of sus-tained yeild.

This block has had heavy recreation use for many years. The summer use has come largely from the upper Okanogan Valley and the Republic area, while the fall deer hunting has attracted people from all over the state. The average annual use approximates 10,000 people, and it is reasonable to expect that with the increase in population in the Columbia Basin and adjacent areas that this use will increase appreciably. b.

Forage and Water Production 

These forms of land use are discussed quite fully in the forest plan. Range management is an important activity on this district and some 4,000 to 5,000 head of cattle are grazed here annually. Little is offered in the way of reservoir values, but use of the water for irrigation is important. -96

3. Cooperation With Other Agencies: 

In 1939, the Biles-Coleman Lumber Company, the largest operator in the Okanogan Valley, owning considerable timber lands and 99-year cutting rights on much more, and having a large investment in logging, milling and remanufacturing equipment, approached all government agencies administering timber lands within the Okanogan Drainage with the proposal that a joint plan of management providing for a cut on a sustained yield basis be worked out. 

All government agencies were required by law to sell their timber to the highest bidder. However, all concurred that a timber management plan was needed, and that timber should be harvested in the best orderly manner. Better inventories were needed, and in 

1948 this was accomplished on the Eastern Division under a cooperative agreement between the Biles-Coleman Lumber Company, the State of Washington, the Bureau of Land Management and the U.S. 

Forest Service. It is hoped that the survey on the Western Div ision can be completed in the near future. As a result of the proposal for a cut on a sustained yield basis, a committee was formed consisting of representatives from the Indian Service, the Washington State Land Department, the Washington State For estry Department, the U.S. Forest Service and a member representing the general public. The committee meets twice annually and serves in an advisory capacity. 

4. Special Problems: 

The large fir-larch volumes offer a problem in cutting methods. 

A study should be conducted to determine the best cutting method to apply to these large overmature and decadent stands. The selective method practiced on the forest is not as readily applicable to the fir-larch types, and it is felt that some clear cut method would be more suitable. Comprehensive, accurate studies should be made as soon as practical to insure that the best form of management is practiced.
