STUDIES Financial Plan 

$1lotment Base

NATIONAL FOREST WORK LOAD AND ALLOTMENT BASE CORRELATED STANDARDS AND CONVERTING FACTORS FOR DETERMINATION OF INDEX JOB LOAD WEIGHTS ON THE NATIONAL FORESTS 

The PM Allotment Base was initially developed in 1931 and comprehensively revised in 1941 through coordinated field studies of the work on the national forests in all regions, exclusive of Alaska and Puerto Rico. Separate analyses are available for those two units. 

In 1946 the Allotment. Base was completely revised on the basis of post war volumes of business and job standards. During 1949-50 it was again revised on the basis of up-to-date volumes of business and a review of standards.

In this 1955 Base revision a larger number of volume of business factors were used to reflect current workloads. Job standards and converting factors incorporate the results of recent field studies reflecting current conditions and practices along with the substantial increase in volume and complexity of work on the national forests since 1950. The 1955 Base contains job descriptions, carrelated standards and converting factors for forest supervisor, ranger, certain sub-ranger jobs, administrative and business management staff and supervisor's office clerical jobs. Work load sussmaries by functions for ranger districts, forests, and regions are included in tabular fora. 

Obiectives and l'afor Uses: 

1. To provide a basis for budget estimates covering PM administrative organization requirements at the national forest level for supervisor, ranger, clerical and business management norma). recurrent work loads. 

2. To develop and maintain a correlated workload base for equitable distribution of the regular PM appropriation among the regions, forests, and ranger districts exclusive of Alaska and Puerto Rico. 

3. To serve as a Service-wide correlated workload base---replacing the need to develop and maintain separate regional and forest bases for allotment of funds and workload evaluations. 

4. To provide statements of all-Service correlated job standards and converting factors for uniform application. These serve as a basis for program evaluation by functional divisions of relative attention given each function for comparisons of parformance with computed and financed workloads. 

5. To provide forests and regions with guidelines and data for determining: (a) organizational adjustments; (b) manning requirements; (c) classification of forests and ranger districts; and (d) arriving at other management decisions. 1955 -1- 

Controlling Principles: 

The Allotment Base is determined by compilation of index jobload weights based on development of converting factors from correlated job standards and application of these time factors to measurable volumes of business common to most ranger districts and forests. 

In the application of this principle of measurement to the job of national-forest management ar related work, it is recognized that: 

1. The basic foundation for measurement of comparative loads among regions is the jobload as it exists on the ground on the national forests and adjoining areas within the zone of national-forest responsibility. It involves work at several organisational levels, namely, forest supervisor; business management and clerical; dietrict ranger; sub-ranger; and certain P&M project work -- irrespective of differences in patterns of organization for the accomplishment of such work. 

2. Use of the Service composite job list, functional management plans, the project work inventory, and similar outlines as a guide in determination of classes of business is essential to a reasonably thorough and comprehensive analysis of the total work load. There arises, however, in every region recurrent and non-recurrent jobs, varying locally in nature, which do not lend themselves to correlated measurement. It is impossible to reflect such jobs in a broad, Service-d de base. Likewise, frequently there are strictly local influencing factors which must be taken into account in local analysis and planning, consideration of which is not practicable nor essential in the 

Service-wide base. 

3. Changes in policy, organization manning, work methods, and new activities and jobs, necessitate periodic review and revision of job standards and converting factors. 

4. Supervisor and ranger (except separately indicated range and recreation aid jobs) level work loads are composed of supervisor and ranger caliber jobs. Accomplishment to established standards requires delegation of many jobs to qualified assistants including functional inspections.

References in job descriptions and standards that rangers and supervisors will do specific jobs indicate work level and responsibility but this does not necessarily mean they personally will perform all jobs listed. Effective national forest administration is dependent in large measure upon delegation of work to the maximum practicable extent and redemption of such delegation by all members of the organisation.

12 

 Method: 

Volumes of business data for 1954 by ranger districts and forests were obtained from the regions. A mumber of adjustments were made in job standards and converting factors based on job and time studies conducted in the field since 1950. New jobs which have developed since the last Base revision were added. Many jobs were redescribed to reflect 1955 requirements and increased complexities. 

Jobs are in the main numbered, described, and arranged in accordance with the composite job list. In this manner Service-wide uniformity in job identification is attained between the Service-wide Base and district and forest analyses and plans. 

Work loads were computed and summarised by ranger districts and forests by IBM punch card methods. In some regions smmaries for forest and district functional totals do not exactly balance due to rounding tenths in IM "hot five" computations. IEM tapes with each job element identified in all all functions for district and forest work loads can be made available to regions. These records provide a detailed breakdown of volume of business, converting factors and Jobload totală 

Conversion factor -- man-hours to man-years-1,800 hours.

Major Adjustments in 1955 Base --- Standards and Converting Factors: 

1. Fire Control --- In revising fire prevention standards and con verting factors recognition was given to the increasing impact of hunters and fishermen (recreation use); to rural populations; and to increased activities of lumbering and mining operations. 

In the suppression standards, recognition was given to the decrease in total elapsed time from origin to control brought about through improved fire fighting methods; better accessibility; advances made in mechanized fire equipment and aerial detection and suppression activities. 

2. Timber Management ---For the Timber Management job, standards were rewritten and new converting factors developed from the 

Service-wide Timber Sale Cost Study conducted in 1954. The entire 

PM time involved in hand ing the timber management job is included in the Base. For other than F&M appropriation jobs, such as 

K-V, Brush Disposal, R&T, ERC, and other non-Pa project programs only non-delegable unit manager time is included. Supervisor staff and ranger assistants'time on those jobs should be financed from appropriate project funds. Total ranger district level production hours (ranger and sub-ranger combined) for the timber sale Job based on F.I. 1954 business (timber cut and sold) have been segregated and listed separately in addition to being included in the total T.M. hours. Timber sale allotments will be adjusted annually on the basis of region, forest, and district timber cut and sold quotas.

Tables indicating ranger district level timber sales, per an hour production factors, together with an entry of the average per man hour costs (including labor, supplies and OR costs) are a part of the workload base. These will prove helpful in calculating district, forest and regional requirements for funds based upon annual timber cut quotas. All elements of the timber sales job at the ranger level are covered in these factors, including cruising, sales preparation, boundary surveys, volume measurement, sales administration, travel and incidental supplies and equipment. Again for jobs involving other appropriations such as "TARP" roads and erosion control, only time for non-delegable unit manager functions has been included. Average cost rates per man-hour have been adjusted to cover 1955 Pay Act salary increases. It is contemplated that the man-hour conversion to dollars will be adjusted to future changes in classification grades of positions required to do a more intensive job. 

3. Range Management. Several basic changes were made in converting factors for range management jobs. The problem (overgrazed) allotment factor has been omitted and replaced by jobs listed below, plus increased allowances per 1,000 acres useable range open to grazing. 

Allowances provide partial recognition to time required in western regions for the new range allotment analysis --- condition and trend job including installation of transects and plots. For the period 

1955-1959 this is considered a recurrent job. Range inspection standards have been modified to enable shifting time from inspection to the analysis job. To give more emphasis to management of stock on the range and human relations aspects, new jobs (preparation of permittee plans, pre-season contacts, cooperation with Advisory 

Boards, other R.M. groups and agencies) were added. Converting factors for range revegetation have been broadened and time allowances increased for both the reseeding and noxious plant controleradication jobs. The range aid job is placed on a broader base to include allowances for trespass and assistance to the ranger in over-all range administration. 

4.Wildlife Management New Wildlife Management job descriptions, standards, and converting factors were developed to place major mphasis upon: (a) habitat surveys, management and development work; (b) cooperative studies and mara gement activities; (c) coordination with other national-forest uses; and (d) big game range administration. Fish and furbearer management jobs and allowances were broadened to reflect current minimum job requirements in these increasingly important activities. 

5. Soil and Water Management. --- Soil and Water Management standards and the basis for applying converting factors have been substantially broadened. In addition to factors for specifically described measurable jobs, a basic district and forest allowance is provided. Forests are classified in four groups based upon Job characteristics and rating factors listed in this section. A time

allowance scale reflects the relative complexity of the jobs for each forest classification. Districts are rated and allowances made in accordance with forest classifications. By these "basic" allowances recognition is given job elements which at this time do not lend themselves to Service-wide correlation. The jobs which this basic allowance cover will be described and standards set for them in a planned Service-wide study of the subject. 

6. Land Acquisition --- Changes in policies relating to the Land 

Acquisition program since 1950 necessitate more thorough revision of job standards and factors than has been possible to date. This function is renamed "Land Ownership Adjustment." in line with program reorientatation from land purchase and exchange to primarily a boundary adjustment exchange program. Workloads in this function are predicated on F.Y. 1954 volumes of business for purchase and exchange cases. Admittedly this is not a reasonably accurate measurement of the 1955-59 job since cases worked on or consummated by districts and forests during that year are not representative of future business. However, total regional allowances may be samewhat close to the time required to handle the jobload during the next year. It is expected that regions will annually shift job allowances (and financing) from those shown for individual forests and districts to those units which will process cases in a particular year. When the new Boundary Adjustment Program firms up, volumes of business, standards and converting factors will be developed for evaluation of workloads and allocation of project funds expected for conducting this program. 

7. Land Use --- Land Use standards and converting factors were revised. 

Frequency standards for special use permit inspections have been modified. New jobs added include mineral reservations and BLM mineral lease (oil, gas, strip mining) applications, records, inspections and reports. Also jobs for supervision of boundary surveys, and the posting and marking of land lines have been recognized in addition to allowances provided in the Timber Management section. 

Work connected with administration of 1955 mining claim legislation is not included. It is intended that correlated standards and allowances for this new job will be developed as soon as job elements, volumes of business items, procedures, etc., can be determined. 

8. Recreation Recreation administration jobs, standards and allowances were broadened to reflect the expanding scope and increased complexity of this activity. New jobs added are: (a) area classification and layout plans; (b) wilderness and roadless area administration; (c) coordination with other uses; (d) cooperation with associated recreation agencies and groups; and (e) supervision of public use of unimproved camp and picnic areas. The basis for applying converting factors was expanded for certain jobs to reflect mandays use at improved and unimproved areas and total use by districts and forests. Recreation aid allowances were similarly revised. 

9. Information and Education --- Information and Education jobs at both ranger and supervisor levels are related to volume of recreation use in addition to the single population density factor applied in the 1950 Base. A minimum allowance or "floor" is provided each diatrict and forest to recognize minimum Jobloads on units with relatively low population and recreation use weights. 

10. Administrative and Business Management -- This section (formerly called Clerical Workload) has been revised in a manner that reflects the staff level operation for this activity as well as the clerical workload. It also provides guidelines for organization which indicate desirable grade levels for the top positions. Computations of jobloads for this activity are based on currently existing jobload (F.T. 1955) (e.g., present dollar expenditures) and will be recomputed each year. The computed staffing represents the hour s required to do a job for all appropriations involved on a specific forest. The cost of doing this total job must be shared equitably by each appropriation and the formulas outlined under this section are designed to insure such equitability. The annual PM allotment for this item will be based on this principle. In all cases appropriation integrity must be maintained by balanced fund financ ing. 

11. Peak Season Factors Work load impacts and problems associated with short field seasons and resultant "peak" work loads in several western regions were restudied. Stronger timber sales, project crews, seasonal forces fire control and ranger assistant manning have aided in relieving ranger peak season overloads. Likewise increased Base allowances for range and recreation aides, the special improvement maintenance allowance for districts with no headquarters guard and new jobs added in practically all functions combine to recognize "peak load" impacts and minimum manning requirements. 

12. Back Country Checks --The great variety of volumes of business on which manning is based should provide time for all national forest areas to be checked on annually by qualified personnel. 

Increased allowances have been made for travel through, and observations in, large "back country" areas. New jobs such as wilderness area administration, checking on possible insect and disease attacks, etc. provide more recognition of the need for back country area coverage. 

13. General -- This is strictly a Pid Base except for Administrative and Business Management (Forest Clerical) section. Development of an All-Fund Base by 1959 is a further objective aimed at making the Base a more valuable management tool. It is planned that standards, converting factors and format for other than Pidi programs will follow the general pattern established for P&M activities. Thus, standards, work loads and allotment procedures can be correlated between regions, forests and districts for FR&T, KV, Brush Disposal, Insect and Blister Rust Control, and similar programs. It is expected that supplements to the 

P& Base will be issued for Land Utilization Projects, the new (1) Boundary Adjustment and (2) Mining Claim programs as soon as correlated standards can be developed. - 3-

TIMBER MANAGEMENT (CONT'D) RANGER JOB (Cont'd) 

Objectives: The objectives of Timber Management are to grow the largest volume and highest quality timber crop in the shortest possible time in coordination with other forest uses; to participate in the protection of the forest from fire, and to protect the forest from insects and disease and other destructive elements; to regulate the harvest so as to provide a maximum continuous supply of useful wood products for social and economic well-being of the people of the United States; to promote the development of new and better utilisation of the timber resource and to secure fair market value for all products offered for sale. 

Job No. 

JOB DESCRIPTION CORRELATED STANDARDS TIMBER RESOURCE PLANS AND INVENTORY 

81-- $1.1-- $1.2--- $2 - Timber Management Units and Plana The job consists of the annual maintenance of the timber management plan or plans and sustained unit plansa in accordance with the provisions of the plans-maintenance section and regional instructions, and to post or supervise the posting of accomplishments (timber cut) currently. 

Timber Stand Data and Statistics --- Ranger will obtain growth, mortality, and other data necessary to effectively maintain currently, and revise periodically, management plans for the working circles on his district in accordance with management-plan provisions and regional policy. 

ADMINISTER TIMBER SALES AND PERMITS 

$2.1 82.11 

32.12 

Prepare Timber Sales

General The preparation of timber for sale should be accomplished within the framework of established policy and regulations, using such methods as are the most economical but which are sufficiently adequate to determine stumpage values, meet the bidders' needs, and sales administration requirements. Sales which include timber on valid unpatented mineral claims located prior to July 23, 1955 (see Manual Title 36, Chap. II, Part 221.5), necessitate that the ranger contact the claimants and secure written waivers to the timber, which may involve also the running of lines and compliance with the claimants' demands for specific cutting practices and specific reservations. 

In addition to participation in road location, specifications, and estimates of costs, the ranger will participate in determination of mileage of timber utilisation roads to be programmed for contract or force-account construction or maintenance, as well as mileage of system and non-system road to be built or maintained by the operator; and he will make recommendation as to roads which will henceforth be considered as system and non-system roads. -Prepare Sales ($2,000 or less) The preparation work on small sales will be confined to those steps necessary to adequately; (1) determine availability of desired products, (2) determine stumpage value and selling price (usually this is done through use of appraisals of comparable timber and site), (3) determine sale requirements and brief purchaser, (requirements such as silvical, fire control, operation practices, duration of sale etc., should be considered.), and (5) handle informal advertising and acceptance of bids to extent of delegated authority (informal advertising should be used in areas where competition is evident.

Prepare Sales (over $2,000)

RANGER JOB (Cont'd) CORRELATED STANDARDS 

The preparation of sales in excess of $2,000 value should include the examination of the area to determine silvical needs and marking practices from which a sale area prescription is developed; estimate of volume and value of timber to be cut; logging costs; stand-improvement needs and costs; slash treatment; road location, specifications and costs; payment unit boundaries; sale-area boundary surveys and and posting needs; rights-of-way deeds.

Prepare Appraisals. Reappraisals, and Reports 

Ranger will contact at least annually selected or assigned sawmills or woodprocessing plants within or adjacent to national-forest boundaries which are utilising national-forest timber. Such contacts are partially made for salespromotion purposes and better utilization of national-forest timber and may be followed up by mailing informational and educational material to operators. 

Rangers will make, under guidance of supervisor and in accordance with Forest and Regional policy, all timber appraisals and reappraisals regardless of size. (c) -The ranger will serve as the supervisor's representative, as delegated, in conducting oral auctions, bidding, and the acceptance of bids. (b) (d) 

The written report on sales over $2,000 should be governed by the need for information upon which to base approval of the sale and should consist of presale reports, appraisal, sample contract, advertisement and prospectus, and other special sale conditions that should be considered and decisions reached in accordance with prescribed regional methods. Appraisals on a comparative basis may be used where practical in specified cases,

Administer Advertised Sales 

-The ranger will take such action as is necessary to insure that: (1) the contract, bonds, etc., are correctly prepared and properly executed; (2) written supplemental instructions in accordance with regional policy, such as administrative instructions, digest of agreement for purchaser, sale fire plan, logging plan, sale maps, etc., are completely prepared in writing, and furnished the responsible administrative officer; (3) L.T.'s and payments are issued and made sufficiently in advance of cutting to maintain satisfactory working balances on deposits (4) boundaries, blocks, fire lines, roads, landings, sets, are laid out and marked in advance of cutting; (5) provisions for all technical operations (marking, scaling, designation, etc.) are made and carried out in accordance with plans and needs well in advance of actual cutting; (6) contract requirements, including progressive cutting, utilization, brush disposal, snag felling, and treatment of soil disturbances, etc., are followed, contractually revised, enforced, etc.; (7) on operations involving assignment of sub-ranger personnel individual training is given and written instructions issued in accordance with regional policy and instructions; (8) reports, studies, cost data, etc., are collected and recorded currently; (9) sales are extended, terminated, modified, closed, etc., in accordance with Manual requirements and regional policy. - 34 -

Ranger will collect additions field data where needed and make written reports on complaints and appeal cases. Preventive action is needed on the part of the ranger to hold the number of such cases to a minimm. 

Administer Unadvertised Sales The ranger will take such action as is necessary so that (1) the contract or permit is correctly prepared with the required number of copies; (2) L.T. 's and payments are issued and made sufficiently in advance of cutting to maintain satisfactory working balances on depsit; (3) the sale is efficiently managed with adequate service to the purchaser; (4) boundaries, blocks, fire lines, roads, sets, etc., are laid out in advance of cutting. 

Technical Operations --- ALL SALES - The most economical method of marking or designation will be selected which meets the silvicai requirements of the type involved as expressed by regional standards and policy. This may vary by (a) class of sale, cutting practices, (b) value of species, (c) live or dead material, and (d) product involved. Methods used range from simple requirements such as designation of all dead wood as removable, or areas from which a certain species or product may be removed, to individual selection of trees and use of ax, paint, or other means for designation. In determining time factors, the time for merking or otherwise designating timber for cutting is combined with the various methods of volume determination such as mark and scale, designate and scale, tree measurement, and sample tree measurement to arrive at a time factor for he combined job. 

Volume Determination — ALL SALES -Volume determination will be accomplished in conformance with regional requirements for the product and species involved. 

The forest officer authorized to make the sale will select the most economical method which will give reasonably accurate results commensurate with the value of the product involved. Approved methods for volume determination are set forth below. In applying time factors, the method most commonly approved for the type involved is used. In areas where consumers! returns are commonly used for certain products, time incidental to volume determination is included in general administration. (1) (2) 

Scaling The need for 100% scaling should be challenged in every case.

Full use of most economical methods such as (1) sample scaling, (2) central point for scaling, (3) bureau scale, (4) consumers' returns, are encouraged when procedures, including provision for checking the accuracy have been established, tested, and proven to give satisfactory results. Specifications as to frequency, place, and minimum amounts necessary to be on hand for scaling should be specifically stated with full consideration of operator needs and the Government objective of reasonable costs, good planning, and efficient operation on a common-sense basis. In cases where additional or extraordinary demands are made by the operator, cooperative scaling deposits should be required of the purchaser.

Tree Measurment --Where satisfactory volume tables are available, where reasonably accurate determination of volume is practical, where cost factors Justify, and where elasticity in the use of time is an important factor: tree measurement may be used as a method of volume determination in accordance with regional policy for products, species, and conditions as stated. Studies, sample plots, check and factor scales, etc., will be used to insure that volume determinations are within a prescribed accuracy and for checking personnel making the original measurements.

Sample Tree Measurement Use of sample tree measurement, as approved under existing regional policy, may be used on individual sales. Area Estimate --Estimating the volume within a specified area, using methods approved under existing regional policy, is acceptable in some situations. Studies, sample plots, check and factor scales, etc., will be used to insure that volume determination is within a prescribed accuracy and for checking personnel making the original estimates.

Administer and Report on Administrative Use Permits 

Administrative Use by Forest Service In all cases where products are removed and used for forest improvements by employees and others, the officer in charge will assure himself that the employee is thoroughly familiar with the rules and regulations for proper forest practices and that the same are applied. 

Administrative Use by Other Agencies --Free administrative use by other agencies will be governed by the same basic standards for administration as stated for commercial sales of comparable size. Form of permit and conditions involved will be in accord with regional policy. 

Records Only sufficient records will be maintained of free and administrative use to enable the officer in charge to report annually the volume involved in accordance with the prescribed forms. $2.26 Administer Free-Use Permits ----- Free use will be governed by regional policy and manual requirements. In areas where demand is heavy, forest and/or district policies will be developed stating: (1) areas or communities from which persons are eligible to receive free-use material; (2) type of material which may be cut (dead, green, species, etc.); (3) areas from which material may be cut; (4) when permits should be issued or where "no permit" free-use areas may be designeted; (5) checking and measurement needed. Ranger will administer free use, in accordance with local policy, at the least possible cost in order to maintain control of the resource and at the same time maintain satisfactory service to the public. In all areas sufficient control will be maintained by the most ●conomical means so that the local officer is assured within a reasonable degree that the proper material is being removed in the proper manner by those eligible to do so, and that the material is not being diverted into commercial chamels. S2.27 $2.28

Prepare and Administer Non-Convertible Sales - Same basic standards for examination, appraisals, advertising, etc., as expressed under sawtimber and convertible products with details varied in accordance with regional and local policies to fit the need of the particular product involved. 

Precutting Exchange Agrement 

Ranger will mark and measure or otherwise designate and measure either trees to be cut or trees to be reserved on the offered land involved in precutting rights under land exchange agreements, and will inspect such lands at least twice annually while cutting is in process. Time allowance for timber cutting activities on the selected land is normally covered under sale administration.
